Semantic Tree-Width and Path-Width of Conjunctive Regular Path Queries \rsuper *
Diego Figueira ORCID:0000-0003-0114-2257, Rémi Morvan ORCID:0000-0002-1418-3405

Abstract
. We show that the problem of whether a query is equivalent to a query of tree-width k k is decidable, for the class of Unions of Conjunctive Regular Path Queries with two-way navigation (UC2RPQs). A previous result by Barceló, Romero, and Vardi [ BRV16 ] has shown decidability for the case k = 1 k=1 , and here we extend this result showing that decidability in fact holds for any arbitrary k ⩾ 1 k\geqslant 1 . The algorithm is in 2 ExpSpace , but for the restricted but practically relevant case where all regular expressions of the query are of the form a ∗ a^{*} or ( a 1 + ⋯ + a n ) (a_{1}+\dotsb+a_{n}) we show that the complexity of the problem drops to Π 2 p \Pi^{p}_{2} . We also investigate the related problem of approximating a UC2RPQ by queries of small tree-width. We exhibit an algorithm which, for any fixed number k k , builds the maximal under-approximation of tree-width k k of a UC2RPQ. The maximal under-approximation of tree-width k k of a query q q is a query q ′ q^{\prime} of tree-width k k which is contained in q q in a maximal and unique way, that is, such that for every query q ′′ q^{\prime\prime} of tree-width k k , if q ′′ q^{\prime\prime} is contained in q q then q ′′ q^{\prime\prime} is also contained in q ′ q^{\prime} . Our approach is shown to be robust, in the sense that it allows also to test equivalence with queries of a given path-width, it also covers the previously known result for k = 1 k=1 , and it allows to test for equivalence of whether a (one-way) UCRPQ is equivalent to a UCRPQ of a given tree-width (or path-width).

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Graph Databases

Graph databases
have gained significant attention due to their ability to efficiently model and manage complex, interconnected data. Unlike traditional relational databases, they model data as entities connected by edges that represent relationships. This structure facilitates the analysis of highly interconnected data, where the topology of the connections is as crucial as the data itself, making them particularly well-suited for use cases like biology, social networks, banking, recommendation systems, and fraud detection. We refer the reader to
[
Bar13
,
Woo12
,
AAB+17
]
for surveys on the foundations and applications of graph databases.

Graph databases
are abstracted as edge-labelled directed graphs

G=\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V}(G),{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}E}(G)\rangle
,
where nodes of
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}V}(G)
represent entities and labelled edges
\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}E}(G)\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V}(G)\times\mathbb{A}\times{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V}(G)

represent relations between these entities, with
\mathbb{A}
being a fixed finite alphabet.
For instance,
Figure

1
depicts a
graph database
,
whose nodes are authors and papers, on the alphabet

\mathbb{A}=\{\text{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.2305,0.3516}wrote},\,\text{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4023,0.8398}advised}\}
.
Edges
x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.2305,0.3516}\scriptsize\text{wrote}}}$}\,y
indicate that the person
x
wrote the paper
y
,
while edges
x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4023,0.8398}\scriptsize\text{advised}}}$}\,y
indicate that person
x
was
the Ph.D. advisor of person
y
.

author
1
author
2
author
3
author
4
author
5
paper
1
paper
2
paper
3
wrote
wrote
wrote
wrote
wrote
wrote
advised
advised

Figure 1
.
A
graph database
with eight nodes and eight edges on a two-letter alphabet.

Being a subclass of relational databases,
graph databases
can be queried by the
predominant query language of
conjunctive queries
, also known as
CQs
,
which consists of the closure under projection—also known as existential quantification—of conjunctions of atoms of the form
x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,y

for some letter
a\in\mathbb{A}
. For instance, the
conjunctive query

\gamma_{1}(x,y)=x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.2305,0.3516}\scriptsize\text{wrote}}}$}\,z\land y\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.2305,0.3516}\scriptsize\text{wrote}}}$}\,z

returns, when
evaluated
on the
graph database

G

defined in
Figure

1
, all pairs of nodes
(u,v)
such that
u
is a co-author
of
v
. Each variable not appearing in the left-hand side of
the definition of a
conjunctive query
(in this example,
z
) is implicitly
existentially quantified.
Note that, to the cost of losing the information of which variable is existentially quantified, every
CQ

can be seen as a
graph database
, where each variable is a node, and each atom is an edge;
hence, we sometimes use
graph database
terminology for
CQs
.

The expressive power of
CQs
is somewhat limited, since

CQs
cannot express, for example, transitive closure.
Since the ability to navigate paths is of importance in many
graph database

scenarios, most modern graph query languages support, as a central querying mechanism,

conjunctive regular path queries
, or
CRPQs
for short.
In particular,
CRPQs
form the core navigational mechanism of the new ISO standard Graph Query Language (GQL)
[
ISO24a
]
and the SQL extension for querying graph-structured data SQL/PGQ
[
ISO24b
]
(see also
[
FGG+23b
,
FGG+23a
]
).

CRPQs
are
defined analogously to
conjunctive queries
, except that their atoms are now of the form

x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y
where
L
is an arbitrary regular language over the alphabet
\mathbb{A}
. For
instance the
evaluation
of the
CRPQ

\gamma_{2}(x,y)=x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.2305,0.3516}\scriptsize\text{wrote}}}$}\,z\land z^{\prime}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.2305,0.3516}\scriptsize\text{wrote}}}$}\,z\land y\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{({\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4023,0.8398}\scriptsize\text{advised}})^{*}}}$}\,z^{\prime}

on
G
yields every pair of persons
(u,v)
such that
u
is a co-author of a
“scientific descendant” of
v
.

Formally, a
CRPQ

\gamma
is defined as a tuple
\bar{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})

of
output variables
, also known as
free variables
,
2
2

2

For technical reasons (see the definition of
equality atoms
) we allow for a variable to appear multiple times.

together with a conjunction of
atoms
of the form

\bigwedge_{j=1}^{m}x_{j}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{j}}}$}\,y_{j}
, where each
L_{j}
is a regular language and where
m\geqslant 0
.
The set of all variables occurring in
\gamma
, namely
3
3

3

We neither assume
disjointness nor inclusion between
\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\}
and
\{x_{1},y_{1},\ldots,x_{m},y_{m}\}

\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\}\cup\{x_{1},y_{1},\ldots,x_{m},y_{m}\}
, is denoted by

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\textit{vars}}(\gamma)
.
Given a
database

G
, we say that a tuple of nodes
\bar{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})

satisfies

\gamma

on
G
if there is a mapping

f\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textit{vars}}(\gamma)\to{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V}(G)
such that
u_{i}=f(z_{i})
for all

1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, and for each
1\leqslant j\leqslant m
,
there exists a path from
f(x_{i})
to
f(y_{i})
in
G
, labelled by
a word from
L_{i}
(if the path is empty, the label is
\varepsilon
). The
evaluation
of
\gamma
on
G
is then the set of all tuples that
satisfy

\gamma
.
For example,
(\text{author}_{2},\text{author}_{5})

satisfies

\gamma_{2}

on the
graph database

G
of
Figure

1
via
the function that maps
x
to
\text{author}_{2}
,
y
to
\text{author}_{5}
,

z
to
\text{paper}_{2}
, and
z^{\prime}
to
\text{author}_{3}
.

The language of
CRPQ
can be extended to navigate edges in both directions.

Consider the expanded database
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}G^{\pm}}
obtained from
G
by
adding, for every edge
x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,y
in
G
, an extra edge
y\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a^{-}}}$}\,x
.
We obtain a graph database on the alphabet
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathbb{A}^{\pm}}=\mathbb{A}\cup\mathbb{A}^{-}
where

\mathbb{A}^{-}=\{a^{-}\mid a\in\mathbb{A}\}
. We then define the syntax of
a
CRPQ with two-way navigation
, or
C2RPQ
, as a
CRPQ
on the alphabet
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbb{A}^{\pm}}
.
Its
evaluation
is defined as the
evaluation
of the
CRPQ
on
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G^{\pm}}
.
For instance, the
evaluation
of the
C2RPQ

\gamma_{3}(x,y)=x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{({\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.2305,0.3516}\scriptsize\text{wrote}}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.2305,0.3516}\scriptsize\text{wrote}}^{-})^{*}}}$}\,y

on the
graph database
of
Figure

1
returns all pairs of
individuals linked by a chain of co-authorship.
It includes
(\text{author}_{1},\text{author}_{3})
or
(\text{author}_{1},\text{author}_{1})

but not
(\text{author}_{1},\text{author}_{4})
.
If a query has no
output variables
we call it
Boolean
, and
its
evaluation
can either be the set
\{()\}
, in which case we say that
G

satisfies
the query, or the empty set
\{\}
. For example,
G

satisfies
the

Boolean CRPQ

\gamma_{4}()=x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.2305,0.3516}\scriptsize\text{wrote}}}$}\,y

if, and only if, the database contains one author together with one paper they wrote.

To simplify proofs, we assume that all the regular languages are described via
non-deterministic finite automata (NFA) instead of regular expressions,
which does not affect any of our complexity bounds.
However, for readability all our examples will be given in terms of regular expressions.
We denote the set of
atoms
of a
C2RPQ

\gamma
by
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\textnormal{Atoms}}(\gamma)
, and by

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\|}\gamma{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\|_{\textrm{at}}}
we denote its number of
atoms
,
i.e.
,
|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{Atoms}}(\gamma)|
.
Moreover, we denote by
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\|}\gamma{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\|}
the sum of its number of
atoms
with
the sum of the size of NFAs used to describe
\gamma
.

Finally, a
union of CQs
(
UCQs
) (resp.
union of CRPQs
(
UCRPQs
), resp.
union of C2RPQs
(
UC2RPQs
))
is defined as a finite set of
CQs
(resp.
CRPQs
, resp.
C2RPQs
), whose
tuples of
output variables
have all the same arity.
A
subquery
of a
C2RPQ

\gamma
is any
C2RPQ
resulting from removing some
atoms
(possibly none) from
\gamma
. A
subquery
of
UC2RPQ
is a union of
subqueries
of the
C2RPQs
therein.
The
evaluation
of a union is defined as the union of its
evaluations
, for instance the following
UCQ

\displaystyle\Gamma_{5}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{5}^{1}(x,y)\lor\gamma_{5}^{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle\text{ where }\gamma_{5}^{1}(x,y)=x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.2305,0.3516}\scriptsize\text{wrote}}}$}\,y\text{ and }\gamma_{5}^{2}(x,y)=x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xleftarrow{\smash{\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4023,0.8398}\scriptsize\text{advised}}}$}\,z\land z\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\color[rgb]{0.9219,0.2305,0.3516}\scriptsize\text{wrote}}}$}\,y

evaluates
to the set of pairs
(x,y)
such that
y
is a paper written by either
x

or their advisor.
We naturally extend the notations
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}-{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|_{\textrm{at}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}-{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}
to
unions
.

Infinitary unions
are defined analogously, except
that we allow for potentially infinite unions. We often use a set notation to denote the union, especially for
infinitary unions
.

For a more detailed introduction to
CRPQs
, we refer the reader to
[
Fig21
]
.
For a more general introduction to different query languages for
graph databases
—including
CRPQs
—see
[
BB13
]
, and for a more practical approach,
see
[
AAB+17
]
.

The
evaluation problem
for
UC2RPQ
is the problem of, given
a
UC2RPQ

\Gamma
, a
graph database

G
and a tuple
\bar{u}
of elements of
G
,
whether
\bar{u}

satisfies

\Gamma
on
G
.
Given two
UC2RPQ

\Gamma

and
\Gamma^{\prime}
whose
output variables
have the same arity,
we say that
\Gamma
is
contained
in
\Gamma^{\prime}
,
denoted by
\Gamma\mathrel{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\subseteqq}}\Gamma^{\prime}
if
for every
graph database

G
, for every tuple
\bar{u}
of
G
,
if
\bar{u}

satisfies

\Gamma
on
G
, then so does
\Gamma^{\prime}
(we will hence reserve the symbol ‘
\subseteq
’ for set inclusion—note in particular that inclusion (of the
UC2RPQs
, seen as sets of
C2RPQs
) implies
containment
, but the converse does not hold).
The
containment problem
for
UC2RPQs
is the problem of, given
two
UC2RPQs

\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
, to decide if
\Gamma\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\Gamma^{\prime}
.
When
\Gamma
is
contained
in
\Gamma^{\prime}
and vice versa, we say that

\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
are
semantically equivalent
, denoted by

\Gamma\mathrel{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\equiv}}\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Queries of small tree-width

It is known that the
evaluation problem
for
UC2RPQ
is
NP
-complete, just as for
conjunctive queries

[
CM77
, Theorem 7]
.
However, queries whose underlying structure looks like a tree—formally, queries of bounded

tree-width
—can be
evaluated
in polynomial time
[
CR00
, Theorem 3]
.
4
4

4

Theorem 3 talks about query
containment
of
CQs
, which is in fact equivalent to the
evaluation problem
for
CQs
. Moreover, the theorem deals with “query width”, but this parameter is equivalent up to a multiplicative constant to the
tree-width

[
CR00
, Lemma 2]
assuming that the database signature arity is fixed.

Tree-width
is a measure of how much a
graph differs from a tree, introduced by Arnborg, Corneil and
Proskurowski
[
ACP87
]
.
For a gentle but thorough introduction to
tree-width
, we refer the reader to

[
NdM12
, §3.6]
. Formally, a
tree decomposition
of a multigraph
G
is a
pair
(T,\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathbf{v}})
where
T
is a tree and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}:{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V}(T)\to\mathcal{P}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textit{vars}}(G))
is a function that associates to each node of
T
, called
bag
,
a set of vertices of
G
. When
x\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b)
we shall say that the
bag

b\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V}(T)

contains
vertex
v
. Further, it must satisfy the following three properties:

•

each vertex
v
of
\gamma
is
contained
in at least one
bag
of
T
;

•

for each edge
u\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{}}$}\,v
of
G
, there is at least one
bag
of
T

that
contains
both
u
and
v
; and

•

for each vertex
v
of
G
, the set of bags of
T

containing

v
is a
connected subset of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V}(T)
.

The
width
of
(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}})
is the maximum of
|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b)|-1
when
b
ranges over

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V}(T)
.

(a)

“Full” representation of
(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}})
.

(b)

“Concise” representation of
(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}})

Figure 2
.

Two different representations of the same
tree decomposition

(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}})
of a
multigraph
G
with six
vertices. The underlying tree is a path with four nodes and each
bag
contains 3
vertices—hence the
decomposition
has
width
2.

We give an example of
tree decomposition
in
Figure

2
:

•

In
Figure

2(a)
, we give the “full” representation of
the
decomposition
: we draw
T
, and inside each
bag

b
of
T

we represent a copy of
G
. Nodes of
G
belonging to
b
are highlighted, while the others are dimmed. Sometimes, we will only write the
name of the nodes contained in the
bag
, instead of drawing the graph.

•

In
Figure

2(b)
, we give a “concise” representation:
we draw over
G
a coloured shape for each
bag
of
T
. This representation is ambiguous—the structure of
T
is not made explicit—and will only be used when no ambiguity can arise.

The
tree-width
of
G
is the minimum of the
width
of all
tree decompositions
of
G
. The
tree-width
of a
C2RPQ
is the
tree-width
of its underlying multigraph.
We denote by
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.49994ptw}_{k\!}}
(resp.
\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathcal{1T\hskip-1.49994ptw}_{k\!}}
) the set of all
C2RPQs
(resp.
CRPQs
) of
tree-width
at most
k
. The
tree-width
of a
UC2RPQ
is simply the maximum of the
tree-width
of its
C2RPQs
.
A
path decomposition
is a
tree decomposition

(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}})
in which
T
is a path. The
path-width
of
\gamma
is the minimum of the
width
among all
path decompositions
of
\gamma
. The
path-width
of a
C2RPQ
and
UC2RPQ
are defined analogously. We denote by
\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathcal{P\hskip-0.50003ptw}_{k\!}}
(resp.
\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathcal{1P\hskip-0.50003ptw}_{k\!}}
) the set of all
C2RPQs
(resp.
CRPQs
) of
path-width
at most
k
. The relationship between these classes is depicted in
Figure

3
:
note that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{1T\hskip-1.49994ptw}_{k\!}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{1P\hskip-0.50003ptw}_{k\!}}
are not explicitly drawn, but correspond to the
intersection of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.49994ptw}_{k\!}}
(resp.
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{P\hskip-0.50003ptw}_{k\!}}
) with the class of
CRPQs
.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.7188,0.1914}\mathcal{P\hskip-1.34995ptw}_{1\!}}
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.7188,0.1914}\mathcal{P\hskip-1.34995ptw}_{k\!}}
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4023,0.8398}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.34995ptw}_{1\!}}
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\color[rgb]{0.2188,0.4023,0.8398}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.34995ptw}_{k\!}}
CRPQs
C2RPQs

.

Clickable taxonomy of syntactic classes studied in this paper.

Similar statements of the following proposition can be considered Folklore (see
e.g.

[
RBV17
, Theorem IV.3]
); however, our inability to find a proof for it with sharp bounds invites us to include a proof.

Proposition 1

(Proof in
Appendix

A
)
.

For each
k\geqslant 1
, the
evaluation problem
for
UC2RPQs
of
tree-width
at
most
k
can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\cdot|G|^{k+1}\cdot\log{|G|})
on a Turing machine,
or
\mathcal{O}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\cdot|G|^{k+1})
under a RAM model, where
\Gamma
and
G
are the input
UC2RPQ
and
graph database
, respectively.

In practice,
graph databases
tend to be huge and often changing, while queries
are in comparison very small.
This motivates the following question, given some natural
k\geqslant 1
:

Given a
UC2RPQ

\Gamma
, is it
equivalent
to a
UC2RPQ

\Gamma^{\prime}
of
tree-width
at most
k
?

That is, does it have
semantic tree-width
at most
k
?

This problem is called the
semantic tree-width
k
problem
.
Should it be decidable in a constructive way—that is, decidable, and if the answer is positive, we can compute a witnessing
\Gamma^{\prime}
from
\Gamma
—, then one could, once and for all,
compute
\Gamma^{\prime}
from
\Gamma
and, whenever one wants to
evaluate

\Gamma
on a
database,
evaluate

\Gamma^{\prime}
instead.

We will also study the restriction of these notions to one-way queries: a
UCRPQ
has
one-way semantic tree-width
at most
k
if it is equivalent to a
UCRPQ
of
tree-width
at most
k
. The
one-way semantic tree-width
k
problem
is the problem of, given a
UCRPQ

\Gamma
, whether it has
one-way semantic tree-width
at most
k
.

Example 1.1
.

Consider the following
CRPQs
,
5
5

5

In this graphical representation,
we interpret a labelled graph as the
CRPQ
defined as
the conjunction of the
atoms
induced by the labelled edges of the graph.
For instance,
\gamma(\bar{x})
is a conjunction of six
atoms
.

where
\bar{x}=(x_{0},x_{1},y,z)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma(\bar{x})\mathrel{\hat{=}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta(\bar{x})\mathrel{\hat{=}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{\prime}(\bar{x})\mathrel{\hat{=}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a(bb)^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ab(bb)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a(bb)^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ab(bb)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b(bb)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(bb)^{+}}

The underlying graph of
\gamma(\bar{x})
being the directed 4-clique,
\gamma(\bar{x})
has
tree-width
3. We claim that
\gamma(\bar{x})
is equivalent to the
UCRPQ

\delta(\bar{x})\lor\delta^{\prime}(\bar{x})
, and hence has
one-way semantic tree-width
at most 2.

Indeed, given a
graph database
satisfying
\gamma(\bar{x})
via some mapping
\mu
,
it suffices to make a case disjunction on whether the number of
b
-labelled
atoms

in the path from

\mu(y)
to
\mu(z)
is even or odd. In the first case, the
atom

x_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a(bb)^{+}}}$}\,z
becomes
redundant since we can deduce the existence of such a path from the conjunction

x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,y\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{(bb)^{+}}}$}\,z
, and hence the
database

satisfies

\delta(\bar{x})
via
\mu
.
Symmetrically, in the second case, the
atom

x_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{b(bb)^{*}}}$}\,z
becomes redundant,
and the
database

satisfies

\delta^{\prime}(\bar{x})
via
\mu
.
Thus,
\gamma(\bar{x})

is
contained
, and hence
equivalent
(the other
containment
being trivial), to
the
UCRPQ

\delta(\bar{x})\lor\delta^{\prime}(\bar{x})
of
tree-width
2.
∎

1.2.
Related Work

On the class
conjunctive queries
, the
semantic tree-width
k
problem
becomes the
coNP
-complete problem of finding out whether the retraction of a query has
tree-width

at most
k
. In fact,
CQs
enjoy the effective existence of unique minimal queries
[
CM77
, Theorem 12]
, which happen to also minimize the tree-width. For
CRPQs
and
UC2RPQs
, the question is far more challenging, and it has only been solved for the case
k=1

by Barceló, Romero, and Vardi
[
BRV16
, Theorem 6.1]
; the case
k>1
was left widely open

[
BRV16
, §7]
.

Furthermore, classes of
CQs
of bounded
semantic tree-width
precisely characterize tractable (and
FPT
)
evaluation problem

[
Gro07
, Theorem 1.1]
.
This result is on bounded-arity schemas, which was later generalized
[
CGLP20
, Theorem 1]
for characterizing
FPT

evaluation
on arbitrary schemas—by replacing
semantic tree-width
with semantic “submodular width”
[
Mar13
]
.

The problem of computing
maximal under-approximations
of
CQs
of a given
tree-width
has been explored in
[
BLR14
]
.
A
maximal under-approximations
of tree-width at most
k
of a
CQ

\gamma
consists of a

CQ

\delta_{k}
of
tree-width
at most
k
, which under-approximates it,

i.e.

\delta_{k}
is
contained
in
\gamma
, and which is maximal, in the sense that for every
CQ

\delta^{\prime}
, if
\delta^{\prime}
has
tree-width
at most
k
and is
contained
in
\gamma
, then

\delta^{\prime}
is
contained
in
\delta_{k}
.

Maximal under-approximations
of a given
tree-width
for
CQs
always exist
[
BLR14
]
and thus, a
CQ
is
semantically equivalent

to a
CQ
of
tree-width
at most
k
if, and only if, it is equivalent to its maximal under-approximation of
tree-width
at most
k
. Our solution to decide the

semantic tree-width
k
problem
for
UC2RPQs
is based on this idea.

While
maximal under-approximations
always exist for
CQs
, this is not the case for the dual notion of “minimal over-approximations”. The problem of when these exist is still unknown to be decidable, aside for some the special cases of acyclic
CQs
and Boolean
CQs
over binary schemas
[
BRZ20
]
.

1.3.
Contributions

Here we solve both the
semantic tree-width
k
problem
and
one-way semantic tree-width
k
problem
for every
k
with one unifying approach.

Theorem 1.2
.

For each
k\geqslant 1
, the
semantic tree-width
k
problem
and the
one-way semantic tree-width
k
problem
are decidable. Moreover, these problems are in
2ExpSpace
and are
ExpSpace
-hard.
When
k=1
, the problems are in fact
ExpSpace
-complete.

In
Section

3
(
4
),
we prove the upper bound for
k\geqslant 2
, by relying on the so-called “
Key Lemma
”, which is our main technical result, and is proven in
Sections

4
and
5
.
The upper bound for the case
k=1
—which was already proven in
[
BRV16
]
for the (two-way)
semantic tree-width
1
problem
—is shown in
Section

7
(
Corollary

7.8
). The lower bound is shown in
Section

9
(
22
).

The
Key Lemma
(
Lemma

3.8
) essentially states that
every
UC2RPQ
has a computable “maximal under approximation” by a
UC2RPQ
of
tree-width

k
and that this approximation is well-behaved with respect to the class of languages used to label the queries under some mild assumptions on it (being “
closed under sublanguages
”). Let us first
explain this assumption before formalizing the statement above (stated as
Section

1.3
).

For a class
\mathcal{L}
of languages, let
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\textnormal{UC2RPQ}}(\mathcal{L})
denote the class of all
UC2RPQs
whose atoms are all labelled by languages from
\mathcal{L}
.
For an NFA
\mathcal{A}
and two states
q,q^{\prime}
thereof, we denote by
\mathcal{A}\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}[q,q^{\prime}]}
the
sublanguage
of
\mathcal{A}
recognized when considering
\{q\}
as the set of initial states and
\{q^{\prime}\}
as the set of final states.
We say that
\mathcal{L}
is
closed under sublanguages
if
(i) it contains every language of the form
\{a\}
,
where
a\in\mathbb{A}
is any (positive) letter such that either
a
or
a^{-}
occur in a word of a
language of
\mathcal{L}
, and (ii) for every language
L\in\mathcal{L}
there exists an NFA
\mathcal{A}_{L}
accepting
L
such that every
sublanguage

\mathcal{A}_{L}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}[q,q^{\prime}]}
distinct from
\varnothing
and

\{\varepsilon\}
belongs to
\mathcal{L}
.

To the best of our knowledge,
all classes of regular expressions that have been considered in the realm of regular path queries (see,
e.g.
,
[
FGK+20
, §1]
) are
closed under sublanguages
. In particular, this is
the case for the class
\bigl\{\{a_{1}+\ldots+a_{n}\}\mid a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{A}\bigr\}\cup\bigl\{a^{*}\mid a\in\mathbb{A}\bigr\}
, which will be our focus of study in
Section

6
. Moreover, even if some class
\mathcal{L}

is not
closed under sublanguages
, such as
\{(aa)^{*}\}
,
then it is contained in a minimal class
closed under sublanguages
—
\{a,a(aa)^{*},(aa)^{*}\}
in
this example.

We can now state the main implication of the
Key Lemma
(whose formal statement requires some extra definitions).

{restatable*}
[Existence of the
maximal under-approximation
]cormuaexistseffective

For each
k\geqslant 2
, for each class
\mathcal{L}

closed under sublanguages
,
and for each query
\Gamma\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{UC2RPQ}}(\mathcal{L})
,
there exists
\Gamma^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{UC2RPQ}}(\mathcal{L})
of
tree-width
at most
k

such that

\Gamma^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\Gamma
, and for every
\Delta\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{UC2RPQ}}
, if
\Delta
has

tree-width
at most
k
and
\Delta\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\Gamma
, then
\Delta\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Moreover,
\Gamma^{\prime}
is computable from
\Gamma
in
ExpSpace
.

As a consequence of
Sections

1.3
and
1
, we have that queries of bounded
semantic tree-width
have tractable evaluation.

{restatable*}
[FPT evaluation for bounded
semantic tree-width
]corfptEvalBoundedSemTreeWidth

For each
k\geqslant 1
, the
evaluation problem
for
C2RPQs
of
semantic tree-width

at most
k
is fixed-parameter tractable—
FPT
—when parametrized in the size of
the query. More precisely on input
\langle\Gamma,G\rangle
,
the algorithm runs in time

\mathcal{O}(f({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|})\cdot|G|^{k+1}\cdot\log{|G|})
on a Turing machine, where
f
is a doubly-exponential function—or
\mathcal{O}(f({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|})\cdot|G|^{k+1})
under a RAM model.

Note that
[
FGM24
, Theorem 22]
shows that the statement above can be improved to have a single-exponential function
f
.

Moreover, we also show that for any class
\mathcal{L}
of regular languages
closed under sublanguages
, if
\Gamma\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{UC2RPQ}}(\mathcal{L})
has
semantic tree-width

k>1
,
then
\Gamma
is equivalent to a
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{UC2RPQ}}(\mathcal{L})
of
tree-width
at most
k
.
Analogous characterizations hold for
k=1
and/or
path-width
, see
Corollaries

7.10
and
8.9
.

{restatable*}
thmclosureundersublanguages

Assume that
\mathcal{L}
is
closed under sublanguages
.
For any
k>1
and any query
\Gamma\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{UC2RPQ}}(\mathcal{L})
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

\Gamma
is
equivalent
to an
infinitary union
of
conjunctive queries

of
tree-width
at most
k
;

(2)

\Gamma
has
semantic tree-width
at most
k
;

(3)

\Gamma
is
equivalent
to a
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{UC2RPQ}}(\mathcal{L})
of
tree-width
at most
k
.

The implications
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{{(3)}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{{(2)}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{{(1)}}}
immediately follow
from the definition of the
semantic tree-width
.
On the other hand, the
implications
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{{(1)}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{{(2)}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{{(2)}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{{(3)}}}
are surprising,
since they are both trivially false when
k=1
. We defer the proof of this last claim
to
Remark

3.14
as we first need a few tools to manipulate
CRPQs
.

The previous theorem, together with the high complexity of
semantic tree-width
k
problem
,
motivates us to focus on the case of
CRPQs
using some
simple regular expressions
(
SRE
) in
Section

6
, where we show that the complexity of this problem is much lower.

{restatable*}

thmthmSemTwSREpitwo

For
k\geqslant 2
, the
semantic tree-width
k
problem
for
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{UCRPQ}(\textup{SRE})}
is in
\Pi^{p}_{2}
.

We then study the problem of
k=1
: at first glance, our proof for
k\geqslant 2
of

Theorem

1.2
does not capture this case, for a technical—yet crucial—reason. In

Section

7
, we explain how to adapt our proof to capture it:
and show the decidability the
semantic tree-width 1 problem
—which was already studied by
Barceló, Romero and Vardi
[
BRV16
]
—and of the
one-way semantic tree-width 1 problem
.

Building on the same idea, we show in
Section

8
that our results extend to
path-width
.

{restatable*}
thmdecidabilitySemPw

For each
k\geqslant 1
,
the
semantic path-width
k
problems
are decidable. Moreover, they lie in
2ExpSpace

and are
ExpSpace
-hard. Moreover, if
k=1
, these problems are in fact
ExpSpace
-complete.

In turn, this leads to an evaluation algorithm with a remarkably low complexity.

{restatable*}

For each

, the

, restricted to

of

at most

is in
paraNL
when parametrized in the size of the query.
More precisely, the problem, on input
\langle\Gamma,G\rangle
, can be solved in
non-deterministic space
f(|\Gamma|)+\log(|G|)
, where
f
is a single exponential
function.

.

.

Interestingly, the proof for

(

We show that a

). In other words, if the original query does not

Finally, we conclude in

.

à la

.

{restatable*}

Assuming
\textup{W[1]}\neq

, for any recursively enumerable class
\mathcal{C}
of

).

1.4.
Conference Paper

The current article is based on the conference paper
[
FM23
]
. The main results for
tree-width

k>1
are essentially the same—though with improved explanations and figures, and we fixed some minor bugs in the proof of the
Key Lemma
. Here we also show how to extend our techniques to tackle the
semantic tree-width
1
problem
(
Section

7
) and we introduce and study the
semantic path-width
k
problems
(
Section

8
). Our very partial lift of Grohe’s characterization of
FPT
classes of queries (
Section

1.3
) is also new.

2.
Preliminaries

Before attacking the statement of our
Key Lemma
in
Section

3
,
we first give a few elementary definitions on
C2RPQs
in this section.
We write
\mathbb{N}
to denote
\{0,1,\dotsc\}
and
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\llbracket}i,j\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\rrbracket}
to denote
\{n\in\mathbb{N}:i\leqslant n\leqslant j\}
.
A
homomorphism

f
from a
C2RPQ

\gamma(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{m})
to a
C2RPQ

\gamma^{\prime}(y_{1},\dotsc,y_{m})
is a mapping from
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textit{vars}}(\gamma)
to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textit{vars}}(\gamma^{\prime})
such that
f(x)\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,f(y)
is an
atom
of
\gamma^{\prime}
for every
atom

x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y
of
\gamma
, and further
f(x_{i})=y_{i}
for every
i
.
Such a
homomorphism

f
is
strong onto
if for every
atom

x^{\prime}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y^{\prime}
of
\gamma^{\prime}
there is an
atom

x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y
of
\gamma
such that
f(x)=x^{\prime}
and
f(y)=y^{\prime}
.
An example of
homomorphism
is provided in
Figure

5(b)
.
We write
\gamma\mathrel{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\gamma^{\prime}
if there is a
homomorphism
from
\gamma
to
\gamma^{\prime}
, and
\gamma\mathrel{\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\gamma^{\prime}
if there is a
strong onto homomorphism
.
In the latter case, we say that
\gamma^{\prime}
is a
homomorphic image
of
\gamma
.
It is easy to see that if
\gamma\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\gamma^{\prime}
then
\gamma^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\gamma
, and in the case where
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
are
CQs
this is an “if and only if”
[
CM77
, Lemma 13]
.

Some intuitions on maximal under-approximations

Given a
conjunctive query

\gamma
,
the union of all
conjunctive queries

that are
contained
in
\gamma
is
semantically equivalent
to the union

\bigvee\{\gamma^{\prime}\mid\gamma\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\gamma^{\prime}\}
. Naturally, this statement borders on the trivial since
\gamma^{\prime}
belongs to this union. It becomes interesting when we add a restriction:
given a class
\mathcal{C}
of
CQs
(to which
\gamma
may not belong) closed under
subqueries
, then
\Gamma^{\prime}\mathrel{\hat{=}}\bigvee\{\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}\mid\gamma\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\gamma^{\prime}\}
is the maximal under-approximations
of
\gamma
by finite unions of
conjunctive queries
of
\mathcal{C}
, in the following sense:

(1)

(finite)
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a finite union of
CQs
of
\mathcal{C}
,

(2)

(under-approximation)
\Gamma^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\gamma
, and

(3)

(maximality) for any finite union
\Delta
of
CQs
of
\mathcal{C}
, if
\Delta\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\gamma
, then
\Delta\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Proof 2.1
.

Only the last point is non-trivial, and follows from the fact that if

\Delta\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\gamma
, then for each
\delta\in\Delta
,
\delta\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\gamma
,
so there is a
homomorphism

f\colon\gamma\to\delta
. The image
\delta^{\prime}

of
f
is a
subquery
of
\delta
, and
\mathcal{C}
is closed under
subqueries
,
so it belongs to
\mathcal{C}
, and hence to
\Gamma^{\prime}
. Since there is a trivial homomorphism
from
\delta^{\prime}
to
\delta
, we moreover have that
\delta\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\delta^{\prime}
.
Hence, for each
CQ

\delta\in\Delta
, there is a CQ
\delta^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
such
that
\delta\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\delta^{\prime}
, and hence
\Delta\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\Gamma^{\prime}
.

As a consequence, we deduce that for each
k\geqslant 1
,
the
maximal under-approximation
of a
CQ
by
a finite union of
CQs
of
tree-width
at most
k
is computable, and hence
we can effectively decide if some
CQ
is
equivalent
to a query of
tree-width
at
most
k
by testing the equivalence with this maximal under-approximation.
For more details on approximations of
CQs
, see
[
BLR14
]
.
Note that interestingly, changing
\Gamma^{\prime}
from

\bigvee\{\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}\mid\gamma\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\gamma^{\prime}\}

to
\bigvee\{\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}\mid\gamma^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\gamma\}

preserves both under-approximation and maximality, but
\Gamma^{\prime}
is now an infinite
union of
CQs
of
\mathcal{C}
.

Unfortunately, these results cannot be straightforwardly extended to
conjunctive regular
path queries
since the previous proof implicitly relied on two points:

(1)

the equivalence between the

containment

\gamma^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\gamma
and the existence of a
homomorphism

\gamma\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\gamma^{\prime}
, and

(2)

the possibility to restrict
\gamma^{\prime}
to its image
\gamma\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\gamma^{\prime}
while
obtaining a semantically bigger query.

These two crucial ingredients is what allows us to build a finite set
\Gamma^{\prime}
from
\gamma
.
For
CRPQs
, the second point still holds, but not the first one.
For instance, the
CQ

\gamma(x,y)=x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,z\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{b}}$}\,y
is
contained in (in fact
equivalent
to) the
CRPQ

\gamma^{\prime}(x,y)=x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{ab}}$}\,y
,
but there is no
homomorphism
from
\gamma^{\prime}(x,y)
to
\gamma(x,y)
.
Our main result shows that to find
maximal under-approximations
of
C2RPQs
,
it suffices to take
homomorphic images
of so-called “
refinements
” of
\gamma
,
instead of
homomorphic images
of
\gamma
itself. The next paragraphs are devoted to
introducing
refinements
and tools related to them.

Equality Atoms

C2RPQs
with
equality atoms
are queries of the form
\gamma(\bar{x})=\delta\land I
,
where
\delta
is a
C2RPQ
(without equality atoms) and
I
is a conjunction of
equality atoms
of the form
x=y
.
Again, we denote by
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textit{vars}}(\gamma)
the set of variables appearing in the (equality and non-equality) atoms of
\gamma
.
We define the binary relation
=_{\gamma}
over
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textit{vars}}(\gamma)
to be the reflexive-symmetric-transitive closure of the binary relation
\{(x,y)\mid\text{$x=y$ is an {\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}equality atom} in $\gamma$}\}
.
In other words, we have
x=_{\gamma}y
if the equality
x=y
is forced by the
equality atoms
of
\gamma
.
Note that every
C2RPQ
with
equality atoms

\gamma(\bar{x})=\delta\land I
is equivalent to a
C2RPQ
without
equality atoms

\gamma^{\approx}
,
which is obtained from
\gamma
by collapsing each equivalence class of the relation
=_{\gamma}
into a single variable.
This transformation gives us a
canonical
renaming from
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textit{vars}}(\gamma)
to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textit{vars}}(\gamma^{\approx})
. For instance,
\gamma(x,y)\mathrel{\hat{=}}x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{K}}$}\,y\land y\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,z\land x=y

collapses to
\gamma^{\approx}(x,x)\mathrel{\hat{=}}x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{K}}$}\,x\land x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,z
.

Refinements

An
atom
m
-refinement
of a
C2RPQ

atom

\gamma(x,y)=x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y
where
L
is given by the NFA
\mathcal{A}_{L}
is any
C2RPQ
of the form

\immediate\immediate\rho(x,y)=x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{1}}}$}\,t_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{2}}}$}\,\ldots\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{n-1}}}$}\,t_{n-1}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{n}}}$}\,y

(1)

where
1\leqslant n\leqslant m
,
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n-1}
are fresh (existentially quantified) variables,
and
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}
are such that there exists a sequence
(q_{0},\dotsc,q_{n})
of states of
\mathcal{A}_{L}

such that
q_{0}
is initial,
q_{n}
is final, and for each
i
,
L_{i}
is either of the form

(1)

\mathcal{A}_{L}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}[q_{i},q_{i+1}]}
,

(2)

\{a\}
if the letter
a\in\mathbb{A}
belongs to
\mathcal{A}_{L}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}[q_{i},q_{i+1}]}
, or

(3)

\{a^{-}\}
if
a^{-}\in\mathbb{A}^{-}
belongs to
\mathcal{A}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}[q_{i},q_{i+1}]}
.

Additionally, if
\varepsilon\in L
, the
equality atom
“
x=y
” is also an
atom
m
-refinement
. Thus, an
atom
m
-refinement
can be either of the form (
1
) or “
x=y
”.
By convention,
t\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a^{-}}}$}\,t^{\prime}
is a shorthand for
t^{\prime}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,t
. As a consequence,
the underlying graph of an
atom
m
-refinement
of the form (
1
) is not necessarily a directed path.
By definition, note that

L_{1}\cdots L_{n}\subseteq L
and hence
\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\gamma
for any
atom
m
-refinement

\rho
of
\gamma
.
An
atom refinement
is an
atom
m
-refinement
for some
m
.
An example is provided in
Figure

5(a)
.

(a)

A
refinement
.

(b)

A
strong onto homomorphism
.

Figure 5
.

Refinements
and
homomorphisms
of
C2RPQs
.

Definition 2.2
.

Given an
atom refinement

\rho=x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{1}}}$}\,t_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{2}}}$}\,\ldots\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{n-1}}}$}\,t_{n-1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{n}}}$}\,y
of
\gamma=x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y
as in (
1
), define
a
condensation
of
\rho
between
t_{i}
and
t_{j}
, where
0\leqslant i,j\leqslant n
and
j>i+1
, as any
C2RPQ
of the form:

\rho^{\prime}=x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{1}}}$}\,t_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{2}}}$}\,\ldots\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{i}}}$}\,{\color[rgb]{0.5352,0.3281,0.8164}t_{i}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{K}}$}\,t_{j}}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{j+1}}}$}\,\ldots\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{n-1}}}$}\,t_{n-1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{n}}}$}\,y

such that
{\color[rgb]{0.5352,0.3281,0.8164}K=\mathcal{A}[q_{i},q_{j}]}
.

Fact 2
.

Every
condensation

\rho^{\prime}
of
\rho
is a
refinement
of
\gamma
, and
\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\rho^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\gamma
.

Informally, we will abuse the notation and
write
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}[}L_{i}\cdots L_{j}{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}]}
to denote the language
K
—even if this language
does not only depend on
L_{i}\cdots L_{j}
.

Example 2.3
.

Let
\gamma(x,y)=x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{(aa^{-})^{*}}}$}\,y
be a
C2RPQ

atom
, where

(aa^{-})^{*}
is implicitly represented by its minimal automaton.
Then
\rho(x,y)
is a
refinement
of
refinement length
seven of
\gamma(x,y)

and
\rho^{\prime}(x,y)
is a
condensation
of
\rho(x,y)
, where:

\displaystyle\rho(x,y)

\displaystyle=x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,t_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{(a^{-}a)^{*}}}$}\,t_{2}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{(a^{-}a)^{*}}}$}\,t_{3}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xleftarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,t_{4}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{(aa^{-})^{*}}}$}\,t_{5}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{(aa^{-})^{*}a}}$}\,t_{6}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xleftarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,y,

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime}(x,y)

\displaystyle=x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,t_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{(a^{-}a)^{*}}}$}\,t_{2}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{(a^{-}a)^{*}}}$}\,t_{3}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xleftarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,t_{4}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{(aa^{-})^{*}}}$}\,y.

On the other hand,
\rho^{\prime\prime}(x,y)=x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,t_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xleftarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,y
is not
a
condensation
of
\rho(x,y)
.
∎

Given a natural number
m
, an
m
-refinement
of a
C2RPQ

\gamma(\bar{x})=\bigwedge_{i}x_{i}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{i}}}$}\,y_{i}
is any query resulting from: 1) replacing every
atom
by one of its
m
-refinements
, and 2)
should some
m
-refinements
have
equality atoms
,
collapsing the variables.
A
refinement
is an
m
-refinement
for some
m
.
Note that any
atom
m
-refinements
is, by definition, also an

atom
m^{\prime}
-refinements
when
m\leqslant m^{\prime}
: as a consequence, in the
refinement
of a
C2RPQ

the
atom refinements
need not have the same length.
For instance, both
\rho(x,x)=x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{c}}$}\,x
and
\rho^{\prime}(x,y)=x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,t_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,y\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xleftarrow{\smash{c}}$}\,y
are
refinements
of
\gamma(x,y)=x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a^{*}}}$}\,y\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xleftarrow{\smash{c}}$}\,x
.

For a given
C2RPQ

\gamma
, let
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\textnormal{Ref}^{\smash{\leqslant m}}}(\gamma)
be the set of all
m
-refinements
of
\gamma
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\textnormal{Ref}^{\smash{}}}(\gamma)
be the set of all its
refinements
.
Given a
refinement

\rho(\bar{x})
of
\gamma(\bar{x})
,
its
refinement length
is the least natural number

m
such that
\rho(\bar{x})\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{Ref}^{\smash{\leqslant m}}}(\gamma)
.
Note that if the automaton representing a language
L
has more than one final state, for instance the minimal automaton for
L=a^{+}+b^{+}
,
then
x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y
is not a
refinement
of itself.
However, it will always be
equivalent
to a union of refinements: in
this example,
x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a^{+}+b^{+}}}$}\,y
is
equivalent
to the union of

x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a^{+}}}$}\,y
and
x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{b^{+}}}$}\,y
, which are both
refinements

of the original
C2RPQ
.

Expansions

Remember that a
C2RPQ
whose languages are
of the form
\{a\}
or
\{a^{-}\}
for
a\in\mathbb{A}
is in effect a
CQ
.
The
expansions
of a
C2RPQ

\gamma
is the set
\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\textnormal{Exp}}(\gamma)
of all
CQs
which are
refinements
of
\gamma
.
In other words, an
expansion
of
\gamma
is any
CQ
obtained from
\gamma

by replacing each
atom

x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y
by a path
x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{w}}$}\,y
for some
word
w\in L
.
For instance,
\xi(x,y)=x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,t_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xleftarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,t_{2}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,t_{3}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xleftarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,y

is an
expansion
of
\rho(x,y)=x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{(aa^{-})^{*}}}$}\,y
.

Any
C2RPQ
is equivalent to the infinitary union of its
expansions
. In light of this, the semantics for
UC2RPQ
can be rephrased as follows.
Given a
UC2RPQ

\Gamma(\bar{x})
and a graph database
G
,
the
evaluation
of
\Gamma(\bar{x})
over
G
, denoted by
\Gamma(G)
, is the set of tuples

\bar{v}
of nodes for which there is
\xi\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{Exp}}(\Gamma)
such that there is a
homomorphism

\xi\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}G
that sends
\bar{x}
onto
\bar{v}
.
Similarly,
containment
of
UC2RPQs
can also be characterized in terms of expansions.

Proposition 2.4

(Folklore, see e.g.
[
FLS98
, Proposition 3.2]
or

[
CDLV00
, Theorem 2]
)
.

Let
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
be
UC2RPQs
. Then the following are equivalent

•

\Gamma_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\Gamma_{2}
;

•

for every
\xi_{1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{Exp}}(\Gamma_{1})
,
\xi_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\Gamma_{2}
;

•

for every
\xi_{1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{Exp}}(\Gamma_{1})
there is
\xi_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{Exp}}(\Gamma_{2})
such that
\xi_{2}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\xi_{1}
.

Note that since an
expansion
of
\gamma
is also a
refinement
of
\gamma
, it also
holds that
\gamma
is
semantically equivalent
to the infinitary union of its
refinements
.

Our approach to proving
Theorems

1.2
and
1.3

and the
Key Lemma
heavily rely on
refinements
. One crucial property
that these objects satisfy is that they preserve
tree-width

k
, unless
k=1
,
as illustrated in
Figure

6
.

Fact 3
.

Let
k\geqslant 2
and let
\gamma
be a
C2RPQ
of
tree-width
at most
k
.
Then any
refinement
of
\gamma
has
tree-width
at most
k
.

(a)

A multigraph together with a
tree decomposition
of
width

k
.

(b)

A
refinement
of the multigraph of
Figure

6(a)
together with a
tree decomposition
of
width

\max(k,2)
.

Figure 6
.

Refinements
and
expansions
preserve
tree-width
at
most
k\geqslant 2
.

Proof 2.5
.

The underlying graph of a
refinement
of
\gamma
is obtained from the underlying graph
of
\gamma
by either contracting some edges (when dealing with
equality atoms
),
or by replacing
a single edge by a path of edges (where the non-extremal nodes are new nodes).

This first operation preserves
tree-width
at most
k
(even if
k=1
),
see e.g.
[
Bod98
, Lemma 16]
. The second operation
preserves
tree-width
at most
k
, assuming
k>1
: if a graph
G^{\prime}

is obtained from a graph
G
by replacing an edge
x_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{}}$}\,x_{n}

by a path
x_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{}}$}\,x_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{}}$}\,\cdots\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{}}$}\,x_{n}
, then
from a
tree decomposition
of
G
it suffices to pick a
bag
containing
both
x_{0}
and
x_{n}
, and add a branch to the tree, rooted at this
bag
,
and containing bags with nodes

\displaystyle\{x_{0},x_{1},x_{n}\},\,\{x_{1},x_{2},x_{n}\},\,\ldots,\,\{x_{i},x_{i+1},x_{n}\},\,\ldots,\,\{x_{n-2},x_{n-1},x_{n}\},

as depicted in
Figure

6
.
All bags contain exactly three nodes, so we obtain
tree decomposition
of

G^{\prime}
whose
width
is the maximum between 2 and the
width
of the original

tree decomposition
of
G
.

For
k=1
, the property fails: for instance the
CRPQ

\gamma(x)=x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a^{*}}}$}\,x

has
tree-width
at most 1 (in fact it has
tree-width
0), but its
refinement

\rho(x)=x\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a^{*}}}$}\,t_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a^{*}}}$}\,t_{2}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a^{*}}}$}\,x
has
tree-width
2.

Fine tree decompositions

For technical reasons—the proof of
9
—, we will use a restrictive class of
tree decompositions
which we call “
fine
’’
6
6

6

This is similar—but orthogonal—to the classical notion of
“nice tree decomposition”, see
e.g.

[
Klo94
, Definition 13.1.4, page 149]
.
. A
fine tree decomposition
is a
tree decomposition

(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}})
in which:

every non-root
bag
can be obtained from its parent
bag
by
either adding or removing a non-empty set of vertices.

(2)

In the context of a
fine tree decomposition
of
width

k
, a
full bag
is any
bag
of size
k+1
.

A
C2RPQ
has
tree-width

k
if and only if it has a
fine tree decomposition
of
width
at most
k
. Indeed, from a
tree decomposition
, it suffices to:

(1)

first merge every consecutive pair of
bags
that contain exactly the same variables;

(2)

between every pair of
bags

that does not satisfy (
2
), add a
bag
whose set of vertices
correspond to the intersection of the two adjacent
bags
.

3.
Maximal Under-Approximations

In this section, we state our key technical result,
Lemma

3.8
, which we will refer to as the “
Key Lemma
”.
Essentially, we follow the same structure as
Section

1.3
:
given a
C2RPQ

\gamma
and a natural number
k>1
, we start by
considering its
maximal under-approximation
by
infinitary unions

of
conjunctive queries
of
tree-width

k
(
Definition

3.1
),
and then show that this query can in fact be expressed as a

UC2RPQ
of
tree-width

k
whose
atoms
contain
sublanguages
of those in
\gamma
(
Key Lemma

3.8
).

For the first definitions of this section, let us fix any class
\mathcal{C}
of
C2RPQs
—we will later apply these results to the class
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.49994ptw}_{k\!}}
of
C2RPQs
of
tree-width
at most
k
.

Definition 3.1

(Maximal under-approximation)
.

Let
\gamma
be a
C2RPQ
. The
maximal under-approximation
of
\gamma
by
infinitary unions
of
\mathcal{C}
-queries is
\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}(\gamma)}\mathrel{\hat{=}}\{\alpha\in\mathcal{C}\mid\alpha\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\gamma\}
.

For intuition, we refer the reader back to paragraph “Some intuitions on maximal under-approximations” at the beginning of
Section

2
.

Remark 3.2
.

Observe that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}(\gamma)}
is an
infinitary union
of
\mathcal{C}
-queries,
that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}(\gamma)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\gamma
, and that for every
infinitary union
of

\mathcal{C}
-queries
\Delta
, if
\Delta\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\gamma
, then
\Delta\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}(\gamma)}
(i.e., it is the unique maximal under-approximation up to
semantical equivalence
).
Similarly, the
maximal under-approximation
of a
UC2RPQ
is simply the union of the
maximal under-approximations
of the
C2RPQs
thereof.

Unfortunately, the fact that a query
\alpha
is part of this union, namely
\alpha\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}(\gamma)}
, does not yield any useful information on the
shape
of
\alpha
—we merely know that
\alpha\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\gamma
. We thus introduce another
infinitary union
of
\mathcal{C}
-queries of a restricted shape, namely
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}(\gamma)}
, in which queries
\alpha\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
come together with a witness of their
containment
in
\gamma
.

Definition 3.3
.

The
maximal under-approximation
of
\gamma
by
infinitary unions
of homomorphically-smaller
\mathcal{C}
-queries is

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}\mathrel{\hat{=}}\{\alpha\in\mathcal{C}\mid\exists\rho\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{Ref}^{\smash{}}}(\gamma),\,\exists f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha\}.

(3)

For a basic example of
approximation
(with no constraint on
\mathcal{C}
),
we refer the reader to
Figure

5
.
The resulting query
\alpha(x,y)
is the
homomorphic image
of a
refinement
of

\gamma(x,y)
. Hence,
\alpha(x,y)\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
if
\mathcal{C}
is, for instance, the class of all
C2RPQs
—or more generally, if
\mathcal{C}
contains
\alpha(x,y)
.

Example 3.4

(
Example

1.1
, cont’d)
.

Both
\delta(\bar{x})
and
\delta^{\prime}(\bar{x})
are
semantically equivalent

to queries in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{2\!}}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma(\bar{x}))}
.
Indeed, starting from
\gamma(\bar{x})
,
we can
refine

x_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a(bb)^{+}}}$}\,z\quad\text{into}\quad x_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,t\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{(bb)^{+}}}$}\,z.

Denote by
\rho(\bar{x})
the query obtained:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\bar{x})\mathrel{\hat{=}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{\prime}_{\textnormal{app}}(\bar{x})\mathrel{\hat{=}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{\prime}(\bar{x})=}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ab(bb)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ab(bb)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces ab(bb)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(bb)^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(bb)^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(bb)^{+}}

Then merge variables
t
and
y
: this new query
\delta^{\prime}_{\textnormal{app}}(\bar{x})

is
equivalent
to
\delta^{\prime}(\bar{x})
. Moreover, since

\delta^{\prime}_{\textnormal{app}}(\bar{x})
has
tree-width
at most 2 and was obtained as a

homomorphic image
of a
refinement
of
\gamma(\bar{x})
, we have that

\delta^{\prime}_{\textnormal{app}}(\bar{x})\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{2\!}}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma(\bar{x}))}
.
A similar argument applies to
\delta
, by
refining
the
atom
between
x_{1}
and
z
instead.
∎

Clearly,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
—whose queries are informally called

approximations
—is included, and thus semantically
contained
, in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}(\gamma)}
,
since
\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\gamma
and
\alpha\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\rho
in (
3
).
In fact, under some assumptions on
\mathcal{C}
,
the converse
containment
also holds.

{observation}

If
\mathcal{C}
is closed under
expansions
and
subqueries
,
then for any
C2RPQ

\gamma
, we have
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}(\gamma)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\equiv}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
.

Proof 3.5
.

Since
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}(\gamma)}\supseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
,
it suffices to show that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}(\gamma)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
.
Pick
\alpha\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}(\gamma)}
. Let
\xi
be an
expansion
of
\alpha
.
Since
\alpha\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\gamma
, there exists by
Proposition

2.4

an
expansion

\xi_{\gamma}
of
\gamma
such that
\xi_{\gamma}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\xi
.
Consider the restriction
\xi^{\prime}
of
\xi
to its
homomorphic image
.
Since
\alpha\in\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}
is closed both under
expansions
and
subqueries
,

\xi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
. Since moreover, by construction,
\xi^{\prime}
is the (
strong onto
)
homomorphic image

of an
expansion
(hence
refinement
) of
\gamma
, then
\xi^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
.
Hence, we have shown that for every
expansion
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}(\gamma)}
,
there is an
expansion
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
with a
strong onto homomorphism
from the
latter to the former, which concludes the proof by
Proposition

2.4
.

Note that
in the definition of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
we work with
strong onto homomorphisms
:
changing the definition to have any
homomorphism
would yield a slightly bigger but
semantically equivalent
class of queries—though having untamed shapes.

Observe then, by
3
, that the class
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.49994ptw}_{k\!}}
of all
C2RPQs
of
tree-width
at
most
k
is closed under
refinements
and hence under
expansions
, provided that
k
is greater
or equal to 2. Moreover,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.49994ptw}_{k\!}}
is always closed under
subqueries
for each
k
.

Corollary 3.6
.

For
k\geqslant 2
, for all
C2RPQ

\gamma
,

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}(\gamma)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\equiv}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
.

Example 3.7

(counterexample for
k=1
)
.

Consider the following query:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma(x)\;\mathrel{\hat{=}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}

We claim that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{1\!}}}(\gamma)}\not\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{1\!}}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
.
First, we claim that
\gamma\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{Exp}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{1\!}}}(\gamma)})
since

\gamma
is an
expansion
of
\delta(x)=x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{abc}}$}\,x
, which clearly belongs
to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{1\!}}}(\gamma)}
.
Then, observe that
\gamma(x)
has a single refinement: itself!
It follows that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{1\!}}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
is finite, and consists precisely of all

homomorphic images
of
\gamma(x)
of
tree-width
at most 1, which are:
