which correspond to the case when the following variable are merged:
\{x,y\}
,

\{x,z\}
,
\{y,z\}
and
\{x,y,z\}
, respectively. Note that
all of these queries are
CQs
, from which it follows that
every
expansion
of a query in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{1\!}}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
is one of the
\alpha_{i}
,
and has a self-loop. In particular, such an
expansion
cannot have a
homomorphism

to
\gamma
. Hence, we showed that there is an
expansion
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{1\!}}}(\gamma)}

s.t. no
expansion
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{1\!}}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
can be
homomorphically mapped

to it. Hence, by
Proposition

2.4
,

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{1\!}}}(\gamma)}\not\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{1\!}}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
. ∎

In general, by definition,

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
is an
infinitary union
of
C2RPQs
. Our main technical result shows that,
in fact,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
is always equivalent to a
finite
union of
C2RPQs
. This is done by bounding the length of the
refinements
occurring in the definition of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
.
For any
m\geqslant 1
, we define:

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\textnormal{App}_{\mathcal{C}}^{\smash{\star,\leqslant m}}(\gamma)}\mathrel{\hat{=}}\{\alpha\in\mathcal{C}\mid\exists\rho\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{Ref}^{\smash{\leqslant m}}}(\gamma),\,\exists f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha\}.

Lemma 3.8

(
Key Lemma
)
.

For
k\geqslant 2
and
C2RPQ

\gamma
, we have

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\equiv}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}^{\smash{\star,\leqslant{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ell}}}(\gamma)}
, where

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\ell}=\Theta({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|^{2}_{\textrm{at}}}\cdot({k}+1)^{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|_{\textrm{at}}}})
.

By construction,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}(\gamma)}
is the maximal under-approximation of
\gamma
by

infinitary unions
of
C2RPQs
of
tree-width
at most
k
. Using the equivalence above and

Corollary

3.6
, it follows that
it is also the maximal under-approximation of
\gamma
by
a
UC2RPQ
of
tree-width
at most
k
.

\muaexistseffective

Proof 3.9
.

The algorithm to compute
\Gamma^{\prime}
is straightforward:
it enumerates
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ell}
-refinements, enumerates its
homomorphic images
,
and keeps the result only if it has
tree-width
at most
k
—which can
be done in linear time using Bodlaender’s algorithm
[
Bod96
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Using the
Key Lemma
as a black box—which will be proven in
Section

5
—, we can now give a proof of the upper bound of
Theorem

1.2
for all
cases
k\geqslant 2
—the case
k=1
will be the object of
Section

7
.

Lemma 4

(Upper bound for
Theorem

1.2
for
k\geqslant 2
)
.

For
k\geqslant 2
, the
semantic tree-width
k
problem
for
UC2RPQ
is in
2ExpSpace
.

Note that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}^{\smash{\star,\leqslant{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ell}}}(\gamma)}
has double-exponential size in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}
,
so testing equivalence of
\gamma
with this
UC2RPQ
yields an algorithm in triple-exponential
space in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}
since
(U)C2RPQ
equivalence is
ExpSpace

[
CDLV00
, Theorem 5]

—see also
[
FLS98
, § after Theorem 4.8]
for a similar result on
CRPQs
without
inverses but with an infinite alphabet. To get a better upper bound, we first need
the following proposition:

Proposition 3.10
.

The
containment problem

\Gamma\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\Delta
between two
UC2RPQs
can be solved in non-deterministic space
\mathcal{O}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}+{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\Delta{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}^{c\cdot{n_{\Delta}}})
, for some constant
c
,
and where
n_{\Delta}
is the maximal number of
atoms
of a disjunct of
\Delta
, namely
n_{\Delta}=\max{\{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\delta{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|_{\textrm{at}}}\mid\delta\in\Delta\}}
.

Proof 3.11
.

The proposition follows from the following claim.

Claim 5

(implicit in
[
Fig20
]
)
.

The
containment problem

\Gamma\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\Delta
between two
UC2RPQs
can be solved in non-deterministic space
\mathcal{O}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}+{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\Delta{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}}^{c\cdot\textrm{bw}(\Delta)})
, where
\textrm{bw}(\Delta)
is the
bridge-width
of
\Delta
and
c
is a constant.

In the statement above, a
bridge
of a
C2RPQ
is a minimal set of
atoms
whose removal increases the number of connected components of the query, and the
bridge-width
of a
C2RPQ
is the maximum size of a
bridge
therein. The
bridge-width
of a union of
C2RPQs
is the maximum
bridge-width
among the
C2RPQs
it contains. In particular, the maximal number
of
atoms
of a disjunct is an upper bound for
bridge-width
.

We provide an alternative upper bound in

Proposition

B.1
(
Appendix

B
),
which also yields a
2ExpSpace
upper bound for
4
.

Proof 3.12

(Proof of
4
)
.

To test whether a query
\Gamma
is of
semantic tree-width

k
, it suffices to test the
containment

\Gamma\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\Gamma^{\prime}
, where
\Gamma^{\prime}
is the
maximal under-approximation

\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}^{\smash{\star,\leqslant{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ell}}}(\gamma)}
given by
Section

1.3
: a double-exponential union of single-exponential sized
C2RPQs
. Thus, by the bound of
Proposition

3.10
(and
Savitch’s Theorem
), we obtain a double-exponential space upper bound.

Moreover, from the
equivalences

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}(\gamma)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\equiv}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\equiv}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}^{\smash{\star,\leqslant{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ell}}}(\gamma)}
of

Corollaries

3.6
and
3.8
, we can derive
new characterizations for queries of bounded
semantic tree-width
.

\closureundersublanguages

Proof 3.13

(Proof of
Section

1.3
)
.

The implications

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{{(3)}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{{(2)}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{{(1)}}}

are straightforward:
they follow directly from
3
.
For
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{{(1)}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{{(3)}}}
, note that
(1)
implies that

\Gamma\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\equiv}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}(\Gamma)}
, and by

Lemma

3.8
,

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}(\Gamma)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\equiv}}\Delta\mathrel{\hat{=}}\bigvee_{\gamma\in\Gamma}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}^{\smash{\star,\leqslant{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ell}}}(\gamma)}
,
so
\Gamma
is
equivalent
to the latter.
Since queries of
\Delta
are obtained as
homomorphic
images
of
refinements
of
\Gamma
, all of which are labelled by
sublanguages
of

\mathcal{L}
, and since
\mathcal{L}
is
closed under sublanguages
, it follows that

\Gamma
is
equivalent
to a
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{UC2RPQ}}(\mathcal{L})
of
tree-width

k
.

Remark 3.14
.

The statement of
Section

1.3
does not hold for
k=1
.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{{(2)}}}\not\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{{(1)}}}
when
k=1
:
consider the
CRPQ

\gamma(x,y)=x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a^{*}}}$}\,y\land y\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{b}}$}\,x
of
tree-width
1,
and hence of
semantic tree-width

1
, and observe that it is not equivalent to any

infinitary union
of
conjunctive queries
of
tree-width

1
—this can be proven
by considering, for example, the
expansion

x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,z\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,y\land y\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{b}}$}\,x
of
\gamma(x,y)

and applying
Proposition

2.4
.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{{(3)}}}\not\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{{(2)}}}
when
k=1
:
by
[
BRV16
, Proposition 6.4]
the
CRPQ

of
semantic tree-width
1

\gamma(x)\mathrel{\hat{=}}x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xleftarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,z\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a}}$}\,y\land x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{b}}$}\,y\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\equiv}}x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{ba^{-}a}}$}\,x

is not equivalent to any
UCRPQ
of
tree-width
1. Hence, the implication is false when
\mathcal{L}
is the class of regular languages over
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbb{A}^{\pm}}

that do not use any letter of the form
a^{-}
. ∎

See
Corollary

7.10
for a similar (but different) characterization
of queries of
semantic tree-width
at most 1.
As an immediate corollary of
Section

1.3
, by taking

\mathcal{L}
to be the class of all regular languages over
\mathbb{A}
, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 3.15
.

Let
k\geqslant 2
. A
UCRPQ
has
semantic tree-width
at most
k

if and only if it has
one-way semantic tree-width
at most
k
.

Lastly, using
Section

1.3
as a black box, we can obtain an
FPT
algorithm
for the
evaluation problem
.

\fptEvalBoundedSemTreeWidth

Proof 3.16
.

First, compute from
\Gamma
its
maximal under-approximation

\Gamma^{\prime}
using

Section

1.3

in single-exponential space, and hence double-exponential time.
Then, evaluate
G
on
\Gamma^{\prime}
using
1
.

This improves the database-dependency from the previously best (and first) known upper bound, which was
\mathcal{O}(f^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|})\cdot|G|^{2k+1})
for a single-exponential
f^{\prime}

[
RBV17
, Theorem IV.11 & Lemma IV.13]
.
We discuss open questions related to this
in
Section

10.2
.

We are left with the proof of the
Key Lemma
. But before doing so, we will need to introduce in the next
Section

4
some basic notions that we will need in the proof, which is deferred to
Section

5
.

4.
Intermezzo: Tagged Tree Decompositions

In this section we introduce some technical tools necessary for the proof of
the
Key Lemma
. Remember that its statement deals with

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}^{\smash{\star,\leqslant m}}(\gamma)}\mathrel{\hat{=}}\{\alpha\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.49994ptw}_{k\!}}\mid\exists\rho\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{Ref}^{\smash{\leqslant m}}}(\gamma),\,\exists f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha\},

and consequently its proof needs to manipulate
homomorphisms

from
refinements
onto
C2RPQs
of
tree-width

\leqslant k
.
The proof will “massage” the
homomorphism

f
and queries
\alpha,\rho
in order to reduce the size of
m
, while preserving (a) the existence of a
homomorphism
between the two queries,
(b) the
tree-width
of the right-hand side, (c) the fact that the left-hand side is a
refinement
,
and (d) some semantic properties of the queries. Our construction will be guided by
the
tree decomposition
of
\alpha
, and more importantly by how
\rho
is mapped onto such decomposition.

(a)

A query
\gamma
of
tree-width
3.

(b)

A
refinement

\rho
of
\gamma
.

(c)

A homomorphic image
\alpha
of
\rho
of
tree-width
2. See
Figure

8
for a
tree decomposition
of
\alpha
(ignoring the dashed blue lines).

Figure 7
.

An example of a
homomorphism

f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-7.39998pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha
.
The
strong onto homomorphism

f
is implicitly defined: it sends the two yellow
vertices of
\rho
on the unique yellow vertex of
\alpha
, and identifies some
blue and red vertices of
\rho
—thus creating purple vertices in
\alpha
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\alpha
be a
homomorphism
between two
C2RPQs
.
A
tagged tree decomposition

of
f
is a triple
(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}},\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathbf{t}})
where

(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}})
is a
tree decomposition
of
\alpha
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}
is a mapping
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{Atoms}}(\rho)\to{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V}(T)
, called
tagging
,
such that for each
atom

e=x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda}}$}\,y\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{Atoms}}(\rho)
, we have that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}(e))

contains
both
f(x)
and

f(y)
.

Figure 8
.

A
fine tagged tree decomposition
of
\alpha
(see
Figure

7
) of
width
2.
(Recall that some bags are omitted for the sake of readability. These bags
are there to make the decomposition
fine
.)

In other words,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}
gives,
for each
atom
of
\rho
, a witnessing
bag
that
contains
it, in the sense that
it contains the image by
f
of the atom’s source and target.
By definition, given a
tree decomposition

(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}})
of
\alpha
and a
homomorphism

f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha
, there is always one way (usually many) of extending
(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}})

into a
tagged tree decomposition
of
f
.

We provide an example of
homomorphism

f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha
in
Figure

7
. Note
that in this example,
\rho
is defined as the
refinement
of a query, and
f
is
strong
onto
—for now this is innocuous, but we will always work under these
assumptions in
Section

5
. In
Figure

8
, we give a
tagged tree
decomposition
of this
homomorphism
. Each
bag
is given a name, written in the bottom left
corner. The
tagging
is represented as follows: if an
atom
is
tagged
in a
bag
, then it
is drawn as a solid bold arrow in this
bag
. Note that by definition, a given
atom
is
tagged
in
exactly one
bag
. For now, blue dashed arrow between bags can be ignored—they will illustrate
Definition

4.2
.

Fact 6
.

Let
(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}})
be a
tagged tree decomposition
of some
strong onto homomorphism

f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha
.
Let
T^{\prime}
be the smallest connected subset of
T
containing the image of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}
.
Then
(T^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}|_{T^{\prime}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}})
is still a
tagged tree decomposition
of
f
,
whose
width
is at most the
width
of
(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}})
.

In the following paragraphs, we extend the notion of
tagging
to paths.
We illustrate this notion in
Figure

8
, where we describe
the path induced by
the blue path of
Figure

7(b)
—which starts at the top-most vertex,
follows the blue atoms, and reaches the bottom-most vertex.
Informally, in the context of a
tagged tree decomposition

(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}})
of
f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\alpha
, given a path
\pi
of
\rho
, say
x_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda_{1}}}$}\,x_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda_{2}}}$}\,\cdots\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda_{n}}}$}\,x_{n}
, the
path induced
by
\pi
, denoted by
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}[\pi]}
, is informally defined as the following “path” in

T\times\alpha
, seen as a sequence of pairs of
bags
and variables
from
V(T)\times{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textit{vars}}(\alpha)
:

•

it starts with the
bag

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}(x_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda_{1}}}$}\,x_{1})
of
T
and the variable
f(x_{0})
of
\alpha
; in
Figure

8
, this corresponds to bag
b^{\text{blue}}_{1}
;

•

it then goes to
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}(x_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda_{1}}}$}\,x_{1}),f(x_{1})\rangle
;

•

it then follows the shortest path in
T
(unique, since it is a tree) that goes to the
bag

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}(x_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda_{2}}}$}\,x_{2})
, while staying in
f(x_{1})
in
\alpha
—in
Figure

8
, this bag is the same as before, namely
b^{\text{blue}}_{1}
, so we do nothing;

•

then, it goes to
\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}(x_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda_{2}}}$}\,x_{2}),f(x_{2})\rangle
in a single step;

•

it then follows the shortest path in
T
(unique, since it is a tree) that goes to the
bag

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}(x_{2}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda_{\lambda}}}$}\,x_{3})
, while staying in
f(x_{2})
in
\alpha
—in our running example, we go from
b^{\text{blue}}_{i}

to
b_{i}
, and then to
b_{i+1}
before reaching
b^{\text{blue}}_{i+1}
;

•

it continues in the same way for all other atoms of the path, ending up with the
bag

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}(x_{n-1}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda_{n}}}$}\,x_{n})
and the variable
f(x_{n})
of
\alpha
.

By construction, note that the constructed sequence
(b_{i},z_{i})_{i}
,
also denoted by
({b_{i}\choose z_{i}})_{i}
, is
such that
z_{i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b_{i})
. Moreover, the values taken
by the sequence
(z_{i})_{i}
are
(f(x_{j}))_{0\leqslant j\leqslant n}
, in the same order
but potentially with repetitions.
Graphically, this sequence corresponds to a path in the
tagged tree decomposition
, where one can
not only move along the
bags
, but also along the variables they contain.
In our example, the
path induced
by the blue path of
Figure

7(b)

corresponds in
Figure

8
to the blue path consisting of both solid and dashed edges.
Moreover, note that a single atom
x_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda}}$}\,x_{1}
of
\rho

induces the path
:

\immediate\immediate\Bigl\langle{\textstyle{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}(x_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda}}$}\,x_{1})\choose x_{0}},\;{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}(x_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda}}$}\,x_{1})\choose x_{1}}}\Bigr\rangle.

(4)

Definition 4.2

(Path induced in a tagged tree decomposition—formal definition)
.

Given a
homomorphism

f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\alpha
and a
tagged tree decomposition

(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}})
of
f
,
the
link
from an
atom

A=x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda}}$}\,y
to an
atom

B=y\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda^{\prime}}}$}\,z
of
\rho
is the unique (possibly empty) sequence

\textstyle{{b_{1}\choose f(y)},\ldots,{b_{n}\choose f(y)},}

where
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}(A),b_{1},\dotsc,b_{n},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}(B)
is the unique simple path from
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}(A)
to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}(B)
in
T
.

The
path induced
by a path

\pi=x_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda_{1}}}$}\,x_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda_{2}}}$}\,\cdots\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda_{n}}}$}\,x_{n}

of
\rho
is the unique sequence

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathbf{t}[\pi]}\mathrel{\hat{=}}\textstyle{{b_{0}\choose f(x_{0})}{b_{0}\choose f(x_{1})}\,L_{1}\,{b_{1}\choose f(x_{1})}{b_{1}\choose f(x_{2})}\,L_{2}\dotsb{b_{n-2}\choose f(x_{n-2})}L_{n-1}\,{b_{n-1}\choose f(x_{n-1})}{b_{n-1}\choose f(x_{n})}}

where
b_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}(x_{i}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda_{i+1}}}$}\,x_{i+1})
and
L_{i}
is the
link
from
x_{i-1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda_{i}}}$}\,x_{i}
to
x_{i}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\lambda_{i+1}}}$}\,x_{i+1}
, for every
i
.

Moreover, given a
bag

b
of
T
and a variable
z
of
\alpha
, we say that

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}[\pi]}

leaves

b
at
z
when
{b\choose z}
belongs to

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}[\pi]}
, and this is either the last element of the sequence
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}[\pi]}
,
or the next element of the sequence has a
bag
distinct from
b
.

For example, in
Figure

8
,

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}[\pi]}

leaves

b^{\text{blue}}_{1}
at the first purple vertex.
Similarly, it
leaves

b_{1}
and
b_{2}
at this same vertex. Moreover,
it also
leaves

b_{2}
at the second purple vertex.

We say that an
induced path
is
cyclic
if it contains two positions
i,j

such that
i+2\leqslant j
and
b_{i}=b_{j}
.
We say that it is
acyclic
otherwise,
meaning that if we visit a
bag
for the first time, we can visit it again at
most once, in which case it must be precisely at the next time step.
For instance, the
path induced
by the blue
atom refinement
in
Figure

8

is
cyclic
. However, the
path induced
by a single

atom
—see (
4
)— is always
acyclic
.

Fact 7
.

If an
induced path

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}[\pi]}
is
acyclic
, for any
bag

b
,
there is at most one variable
z
of
\alpha
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}[\pi]}

leaves

b
at
z
.

Lastly, we define a
fine tagged tree decomposition
of
f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\alpha

to be a
tagged tree decomposition
of
f
that is also a
fine tree decomposition
of
\alpha
.
We abuse the notation and talk about the
fine tagged tree decomposition
of a
C2RPQ

\gamma

to talk about the
fine tagged tree decomposition
of the identity
homomorphism

\mathrm{id}\colon\gamma\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\gamma
.

One of the key properties of
fine tagged tree decompositions
is that in any of its
non-branching paths
—
i.e.
paths in
T
whose non-extremal
bags
have degree exactly 2—, at least half of the
bags
are
non-full
,
i.e.
they
contain at most
k
variables
7
7

7

Recall
that in a decomposition of
width

k
,
bags
are allowed to contain at most
k+1
variables.
.
Such
bags
will prove useful in the next section because of the following property.

Figure 9
.
The query

\gamma\land\gamma^{\prime}\land\delta
(top)
and one of its
fine tagged tree decomposition

of
width
at most
k
(bottom).

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
be
C2RPQs
,
and
(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}})
—resp.
(T^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}^{\prime})
—be
a
fine tagged tree decomposition
of width
k
of
\gamma
—resp. of
\gamma^{\prime}
.
Let
b,b^{\prime}
be leaves of
T
and
T^{\prime}
respectively, such that
b
and
b^{\prime}
are
non-full bags

of the same cardinality,
and let
Z={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b)\cap{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}(b^{\prime})
. In particular, we have

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b)=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\cup Z\;\text{ and }\;{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}(b^{\prime})=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\}\cup Z,

from some variables s.t. the
x_{i}
’s are disjoint from the
y_{i}
’s.
Assume moreover that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textit{vars}}(\gamma)\cap{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textit{vars}}(\gamma^{\prime})\subseteq Z
.
Then, for any conjunction
\delta
of
atoms
the form:

•

x_{i}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y_{i}
for some
i\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\llbracket}1,n{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\rrbracket}
,

•

x_{i}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,z
for some
i\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\llbracket}1,n{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\rrbracket}
and
z\in Z
,

•

z\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y_{i}
for some
i\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\llbracket}1,n{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\rrbracket}
and
z\in Z
,

•

z\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,z^{\prime}
for some
z,z^{\prime}\in Z
,

the query
\gamma\land\gamma^{\prime}\land\delta

has a
fine tagged tree decomposition

of
width

k
in which the length of the longest
non-branching path
is smaller
than the sum of the longest
non-branching paths
of
T
and of
T^{\prime}
, plus
2n
.

The proof of
Proposition

4.3
is elementary and illustrated in
Figure

9
.

Proof 4.4
.

We connect
T
with
T^{\prime}
with
2n\leqslant 2k
bags:
start from
b_{0}\mathrel{\hat{=}}b
, which contains
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\cup Z
. Then create
the following bags:

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b_{1})\mathrel{\hat{=}}\{x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}\cup\{y_{1}\}\cup Z={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b_{0})\cup\{y_{1}\}
,

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b_{2})\mathrel{\hat{=}}\{x_{2},\ldots,x_{n}\}\cup\{y_{1}\}\cup Z={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b_{1})\smallsetminus\{x_{1}\}
,

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b_{2i-1})\mathrel{\hat{=}}\{x_{i},\ldots,x_{n}\}\cup\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{i}\}\cup Z={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b_{2i-2})\cup\{y_{i}\}
,

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b_{2i})\mathrel{\hat{=}}\{x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\cup\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{i}\}\cup Z={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b_{2i-1})\smallsetminus\{x_{i}\}

for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, and observe that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b_{2n})={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}(b^{\prime})
.
Then,
tag
every atom of
\delta
in the first bag of

\langle b_{1},\ldots,b_{2n-1}\rangle
containing both variables of the
atom
.
Such a bag always exists:

•

an
atom
of the form
x_{i}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,z
is
tagged
in
b_{0}
;

•

an
atom
of the form
z\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,z^{\prime}
is
tagged
in
b_{0}
;

•

an
atom
of the form
x_{i}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y_{i}
is
tagged
in
b_{2i-1}
;

•

an
atom
of the form
z\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y_{i}
is
tagged
in
b_{2i-1}
.

Observe that the decomposition obtained is indeed a
fine tagged tree decomposition
:
in particular, it satisfies that for each variable
t
, the set of all
b\in T

containing
t
is a connected subtree of
T
, thanks to the assumption that

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textit{vars}}(\gamma)\cap{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textit{vars}}(\gamma^{\prime})\subseteq Z
.

5.
Key Lemma: Maximal Under Approximations are Semantically Finite

We can now start to describe the constructions used to prove the
Key Lemma

3.8
. Given a fixed
C2RPQ

\gamma
and a fixed
k\geqslant 1
,
we call a
trio
any triple
(\alpha,\rho,f)
such that

\alpha\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.49994ptw}_{k\!}}
,

\rho\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{Ref}^{\smash{}}}(\gamma)
and

f
is a
strong onto homomorphism
from
\rho
to
\alpha
.
For clarity, we will denote such a
trio
by simply “
f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha
”.
Using this terminology, in order to prove
Lemma

3.8
, it is sufficient (and necessary) to show that:

for every
trio

f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha
,
there exists another
trio

f^{\prime}\colon\rho^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha^{\prime}

s.t.

\alpha\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\alpha^{\prime}
and
\rho^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{Ref}^{\smash{\leqslant{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ell}}}}(\gamma)
.

Remark 5.1
.

Note that this section does not use the fact that
k\geqslant 2
. In particular,

Lemma

3.8
holds for
k=1
. However,

Corollary

3.6
does not apply,
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{1\!}}}(\gamma)}
(which we are interested in)
is not equivalent to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{1\!}}}^{\smash{\star}}(\gamma)}
(which is shown to be computable by
Lemma

3.8
). We discuss this case in further details in
Section

7
.

5.1.
Local Acyclicity

Our first construction, which will ultimately allow us to bound the size of
atom refinements
,
shows that we can assume
w.l.o.g.
that they
induce

acyclic paths
in a
fine tagged
tree decomposition
of
f
.

Lemma 8
.

For any
trio

f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha
, there exists a
trio

f^{\prime}\colon\rho^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha^{\prime}
and a
fine tagged tree decomposition

(T^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}^{\prime})
of
width
at most

k
of
f^{\prime}
such that

\alpha\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\alpha^{\prime}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\rho^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|_{\textrm{at}}}\leqslant{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\rho{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|_{\textrm{at}}}
and every
atom refinement
of

\rho^{\prime}

induces
an
acyclic path
in the tree
T^{\prime}
, in which case
we say that
(T^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}^{\prime})
is
locally acyclic

w.r.t.
f^{\prime}
.

Figure 10
.

Changing the blue
atom refinement
of
\gamma
(see
Figure

8
)
so that it induces an
acyclic path
.

Note that the fact that
f^{\prime}
is a
trio
implies in particular that

\rho^{\prime}
is a
refinement
of
\gamma
.
The construction behind
8
is
illustrated in
Figure

10
.

{nota}

When two
bags
are linked by a dashed
edge (as in
Figures

8
and
10
), it means
that there is another
bag
in between them, which is there
to ensure the fact that the
decomposition
is
fine
.
The vertices contained in this extra bag are exactly the intersection of
the vertices contained by its two neighbours, and no atom is tagged inside.

Proof 5.2

(Informal proof of
8
)
.

Start with a
trio

f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha
, and let
(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}})
be a
fine tagged tree decomposition
of

f
. Consider an
atom refinement

\pi\mathrel{\hat{=}}z_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{1}}}$}\,z_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{2}}}$}\,\cdots\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{n}}}$}\,z_{n}

in
\rho
of some atom
x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y
(with
z_{0}\mathrel{\hat{=}}x
and
z_{n}\mathrel{\hat{=}}y
),
and assume that it
induces

a
cyclic path
in
T
— see e.g.
Figure

8
. It means that some variables
z_{i}
and
z_{j}
are mapped by
f

to the same bag of
T
, somewhere along the path
induced
by
\pi
. It suffices then to
condense

\rho

by replacing the
atoms

z_{i}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{i+1}}}$}\,\cdots\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{j}}}$}\,z_{j}
by a single

atom

z_{i}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}[}L_{i+1}\cdots L_{j}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}]}}}$}\,z_{j}
.
We thus obtain a new
refinement

\rho^{\prime}
of
\gamma
.
Then define
\alpha^{\prime}
be simply adding an atom
f(z_{i})\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{i+1}\cdots L_{j}}}$}\,f(z_{j})
.
The definitions of
f^{\prime}
and
(T^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}^{\prime})
are then straightforward—potentially,

\alpha^{\prime}
should be restricted to the image of
f^{\prime}\colon\rho^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\alpha^{\prime}
so that
f^{\prime}
is still
strong onto
by using
6
. Crucially,
\alpha\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\alpha^{\prime}
, and
\alpha^{\prime}
still has
tree-width
at most
k

since we picked
f(z_{i})
and
f(z_{j})
so that they belonged to the same bag of
T
: therefore, adding an
atom
between them is innocuous.
We then iterate this construction for every
atom refinement
.

Figure

10
shows the
fine tagged tree decomposition

(T^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}^{\prime})

obtained by applying the previous construction to the
decomposition

(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}})
of
Figure

8
for the blue
atom refinement
,
followed by applying
6
.
In
Figure

8
, the
induced path
was
leaving
the
bag

b_{2}
both
at the first and at the second purple vertex. This leads in
Figure

10

to a new atom between these vertices. The same phenomenon happens to
bags

b_{3},\ldots,b_{n-1}
.
Lastly, note that because the
atoms
tagged in
bags

b^{\text{blue}}_{2},\ldots,b^{\text{blue}}_{n-1}
are not in the image of
f^{\prime}
,
these bags were removed by
6
.

Proof 5.3

(Formal proof of
8
)
.

Let
\pi
be an
atom refinement
in
\rho
that induce a
cyclic path
in
T
,
say

\pi=z_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{1}}}$}\,z_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{2}}}$}\,\dotsb\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{n-1}}}$}\,z_{n-1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{n}}}$}\,z_{n}.

In order to build the
trio

f^{\prime}\colon\rho^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha^{\prime}

and a
fine tagged tree decomposition

T^{\prime}
of
f^{\prime}
of width at most
k
,
we will mainly use the fact that if two vertices
(u,v)
of some graph
G

belong to the same bag of a
tree decomposition

(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}})
of
G
, then

(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}})
is still also a
tree decomposition
of the graph obtained by adding an edge from
u
to
v
.

By definition, the
induced path

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}[\pi]}=\bigl({b_{i}\choose x_{i}}\bigr)_{i}

is of the form

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}[\pi]}=\textstyle{\Bigl\langle{b_{i_{0}}\choose f(z_{0})},{b_{i_{0}+1}\choose f(z_{1})},\ldots,{b_{i_{1}}\choose f(z_{1})},{b_{i_{1}+1}\choose f(z_{2})},\ldots,{b_{i_{n-1}}\choose f(z_{n-1})},{b_{i_{n-1}+1}\choose f(z_{n})}\Bigr\rangle},

where
i_{0}\mathrel{\hat{=}}0
, and for each
l
,
b_{i_{l}}=b_{i_{l}+1}
.
Since it is not
acyclic
, there exists
(j,j^{\prime})
such that

j+2\leqslant j^{\prime}
and
b_{j}=b_{j^{\prime}}
.
Let
n(j)
(resp.
n(j^{\prime})
) denote the unique index such that

i_{n(j)-1}<j\leqslant i_{n(j)}
(resp.
i_{n(j^{\prime})-1}<j^{\prime}\leqslant i_{n(j^{\prime})}
).
In particular, we have
f(z_{n(j)})\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b_{j})

and
f(z_{n(j^{\prime})})\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b_{j^{\prime}})
.
We claim that
n(j)<n(j^{\prime})
—otherwise, we would have twice the same
bag in a
link
, which would contradict the fact that it is a simple path in
T
.

We can then define

\pi^{\prime}\mathrel{\hat{=}}t_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{1}}}$}\,\dotsb\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{n(j)}}}$}\,t_{n(j)}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{K}}$}\,t_{n(j^{\prime})}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{n(j^{\prime})+1}}}$}\,\dotsb\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{n}}}$}\,t_{n},

where
K\mathrel{\hat{=}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}[}L_{n(j)+1}\cdots L_{n(j^{\prime})}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}]}
(see
Definition

2.2
)
and let
\rho^{\prime}
be the query obtained from
\rho
by replacing
\pi
with
\pi^{\prime}
.
Then, define
\alpha^{\prime}
to be the query obtained from
\alpha
by adding an
atom

f(z_{n(j)})\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{K}}$}\,f(z_{n(j^{\prime})})
,
so that by construction, we have
\alpha\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\alpha^{\prime}
,
that
\rho^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{Ref}^{\smash{}}}(\gamma)
with
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\rho^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|_{\textrm{at}}}\leqslant{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\rho{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|_{\textrm{at}}}
and

f
induces a
homomorphism

f^{\prime}\colon\rho^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\alpha^{\prime}
.

We must then build a
tagged tree decomposition

(T^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}^{\prime})

of
f^{\prime}
.
First, we restrict
\alpha^{\prime}
to be the image of

f^{\prime}\colon\rho^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\alpha^{\prime}
, in order to obtain a
strong onto homomorphism
.
Then, starting from the
tagged tree decomposition

(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}})
of
f
, restrict
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}
to the atoms
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{Atoms}}(\rho^{\prime})\smallsetminus{\{z_{n(j)}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{K}}$}\,z_{n(j^{\prime})}\}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{Atoms}}(\rho)
,
and tag the atom
z_{n(j)}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{K}}$}\,z_{n(j^{\prime})}
to the bag

b_{j}=b_{j^{\prime}}
. This
tree decomposition
has the same
width
as
T
.
Then, apply
6
to get rid of potentially useless
bags
.

Observe then that the
path induced
by

\pi^{\prime}
in
(T^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}^{\prime})
is simply

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}^{\prime}[\pi^{\prime}]}=\textstyle{\Bigl\langle{b_{i_{0}}\choose f(z_{0})},{b_{i_{0}+1}\choose f(z_{1})},\ldots,{b_{j}\choose f(z_{n(j)})},{b_{j^{\prime}}\choose f(z_{n(j^{\prime})})},\ldots,{b_{i_{n-1}}\choose f(z_{n-1})},{b_{i_{n-1}+1}\choose f(z_{n})}\Bigr\rangle}

and thus
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}^{\prime}[\pi^{\prime}]}

is strictly shorter than
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}[\pi]}
since
j+2\leqslant j^{\prime}
,
by definition of these indices.
Finally, observe that if
(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}})
is
fine

then so is
(T^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}^{\prime})
.

Overall, we built
f^{\prime}\colon\rho^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha^{\prime}
together with a
fine tagged tree decomposition

(T^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}^{\prime})
of
width
at most
k

where
\alpha\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\alpha^{\prime}
(by
2
),
and
\rho^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{Ref}^{\smash{}}}(\gamma)
is such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\rho^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|_{\textrm{at}}}\leqslant{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\rho{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|_{\textrm{at}}}
, and
for each atom of
\gamma
, the
refinement
of this atom in
\rho
is exactly the
same as the
refinement
of this atom in
\rho^{\prime}
except possibly for one atom,
for which the path induced in
T^{\prime}
by its
refinement
in
\rho^{\prime}
is strictly shorter than the
path induced
in
T
by its
refinement
in
\rho
.
After iterating this construction as many times as needed, we obtain
a
trio
as in the conclusion of
8
, which concludes our proof.

5.2.
Short Paths

Ultimately,
8
will allow us to give a bound on the number of
leaves of a
fine tagged tree decomposition
of a
trio
. The following claim—which is
significantly more technical than the foregoing—will give us a bound on the height of
a
decomposition
.

Lemma 9
.

Let
f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha
be a
trio
and
(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}})
be a
locally acyclic

fine tagged tree decomposition
of
width
at most
k
of
f
.
Then there is a
trio

f^{\prime}\colon\rho^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha^{\prime}
and a
fine tagged tree decomposition

(T^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}^{\prime})
of width at most
k
of
f^{\prime}
such that:

•

\alpha\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\alpha^{\prime}
,

•

(T^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}^{\prime})
is
locally acyclic
w.r.t.
f^{\prime}
, and

•

the length of the longest
non-branching path
in
T
is at most

\mathcal{O}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|_{\textrm{at}}}\cdot(k+1)^{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|_{\textrm{at}}}})
.

To prove
9
, we will try to find, in a long
non-branching path
,
some kind of shortcut. The piece of information that is relevant to finding this shortcut
is what we call the
profile
of a
bag
.

Figure 11
.

Profiles
of the
bags
in the
non-branching path
between
b_{1}
and
b_{n}
in the
fine tagged tree decomposition
obtained from
Figure

8
after applying

8
and
6
to both the
red and blue
atom refinements
.

Definition 5.4

(Types and Profiles)
.

Given a
trio

f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha
and a
fine tagged tree decomposition

(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}})
of
f
, for each
bag

b
of
T
, we say that:

•

b
is “
atomic
” if there is at least one atom
e\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}^{-1}[b]

and at least one variable
x
of
e
such that
x\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textit{vars}}(\gamma)
, i.e., the atom
e
is not in the ‘middle’ part of an
atom refinement
;

•

otherwise, when
b
is
non-atomic
, we assign to each variable

z\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b)\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V}(\alpha)
a
type

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\textnormal{type}}_{z}^{b}\mathrel{\hat{=}}\bigl\{x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y\text{ {\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}atom} of $\gamma$}\;\big|\;{}

the path
induced
by the
atom refinement

\displaystyle\text{of $x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y$ in $\rho$ {\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}leaves} $b$ at $z$}\bigr\},

where each
type
is potentially the empty set.
Then the
profile
of
b
is the multiset of the types of
z

when
z
ranges over
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b)
.

Note that
\rho
and
\alpha
can have arbitrarily more
atoms
than the original query

\gamma
, and so the numbers of
bags
in
T
can be arbitrarily high. However,
only few of them can be
atomic
: an
atom refinement
of
atom
of
\gamma

contains at most two
atoms
with a variable from
\gamma
—namely the
first and the last
atom
in the
refinement
.

Fact 10
.

There is at most
2{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|_{\textrm{at}}}

atomic bags
in
T
.

Consider the
fine tree decomposition
of
Figure

10
, and now apply the construction of
8
to the red
atom refinement
, followed by
6
. We now obtain a
non-branching path

between
bags

b_{1}
and
b_{n}
. We depict it in
Figure

11
:
the implicit
bags
, hidden behind the dashed edges in
Figure

10
(see
Section

5.1
), are made explicit in this new figure, and, moreover, the rest of the
fine tree decomposition
is not drawn.
Lastly, for each
bag
, we indicate if it is
full
and if it is
atomic
; when it is not
atomic
,
we provide the
profile
of the
bag
.

The rest of the proof consists in two parts.
First, we show that if two
non-atomic bags

b
and
b^{\prime}

occurring in some
non-branching path

of
T
have the same
profile
, then we can essentially replace
the path between
b
and
b^{\prime}
by a path of constant length (
Section

5.2
). And second, we show that in every sufficiently long
non-branching path

we can find
b
and
b^{\prime}
satisfying the aforementioned property: this
part simply relies on an enhanced “pigeonhole principle” (
11
).

{clm}

[]

Let
f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha
be a
trio
, and consider a

fine tagged tree decomposition
of
f
which is
locally acyclic
.
Suppose there are two
bags

b
and
b^{\prime}
such that:

(1)

they contain at most
k
nodes (
i.e.
, not
full

bags
),

(2)

they have the same
profile
,

(3)

there is a
non-branching path
in
T
between these
bags
, and

(4)

no
bags
of the path between
b
and
b^{\prime}
(both included) are
atomic
.

Then, there exists a
trio

f^{\prime}\colon\rho^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha^{\prime}
and a

fine tagged tree decomposition
of
f^{\prime}
of
width
at most
k

that can be obtained by replacing the
non-branching path
between
b
and
b^{\prime}

in the
fine tagged tree decomposition
of

f
by another
non-branching path
with at most

2k+1

bags
, such that
\alpha\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\alpha^{\prime}
.

The proof of
Section

5.2
relies on the definition of
profile
, which
was specifically designed so that we can
condense
every
refinement
between
b
and
b^{\prime}
,
while preserving every needed property of the
trio
.
We give first an informal and then a formal proof of
Section

5.2
, which are
illustrated in
Figure

12
.

(a)

An
approximation

\alpha
.

(c)

The resulting
approximation

\alpha^{\prime}
.

(b)

A
fine tagged tree decomposition
of
f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-7.39998pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha

together with the
path induced
by three
atom refinements

of
\rho
(in red, green & blue).

Figure 12
.
A
trio

f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.83246pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-7.39998pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha
—where
\rho
and
f
are implicit—
together with one of its
fine tagged tree decomposition

(
Figures

12(a)
and
12(b)
).
There are two non-
full bags
in the path with the same
profile
, and thus the query
\alpha
can be simplified to
\alpha^{\prime}

(see
Figure

12(c)
)
by applying
condensations
to the
atom refinements
involved.

Proof 5.5

(Informal proof of
Section

5.2
)
.

If
b
and
b^{\prime}
have the same
profile
, then in particular they have the same cardinality
m
,
which is smaller or equal to
k
by assumption.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b)=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b^{\prime})=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\}
be such that:

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{type}}^{b}_{x_{i}}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{type}}^{b^{\prime}}_{y_{i}}
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant m
.
Note that the
x_{i}
’s don’t need to be distinct from the
y_{i}
’s.
Essentially, we can then
condense

every
atom refinement
in
\rho
of some
atom
occurring in a set
of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{type}}^{b}_{x_{i}}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{type}}^{b^{\prime}}_{y_{i}}
for some
i
.
At this point, bags strictly comprised between

b
and
b^{\prime}
are discarded, and so are variables of
\alpha
that do not occur anywhere else.
We are left with two halves of a
fine tagged tree decomposition
that we need to merge,
which can easily be done by using
Proposition

4.3
.
The construction makes use of some crucial ingredients to guarantee its correctness.

•

First, an
atom

y\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y^{\prime}
of
\gamma
cannot occur
in two different
types
, allowing us to do the
condensation
of each
atom refinement
independently—this property is guaranteed by the fact that we started
with a
locally acyclic

fine tagged tree decomposition
, so an
atom
of
\gamma
cannot leave a given
bag
at two different variables, by

7
.

•

Second, this
condensation
forces us to add new
atoms
in
\alpha
(to preserve the
existence of a
homomorphism
from the
refinement
to the approximation)
from some variables of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b)
to some variables of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b^{\prime})
,
but we only add edges from
x_{i}
to
y_{i}
, and never from
x_{i}
to
y_{j}
with

i\neq j
. This allows us to preserve the
tree-width
of the approximation
by using
Proposition

4.3
.

Proof 5.6

(Formal proof of
Section

5.2
)
.

Let

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b)=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}\}\text{ and }{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b^{\prime})=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\}

be as in the informal proof.
Note that given an
atom

x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y
of
\gamma
and
a
bag
, there is at most
one variable of
\alpha
s.t.
x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y
is in the
type
of this variable
at this bag, by
7
.

For every
atom

x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y
of
\gamma
, let

\pi(x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y)\mathrel{\hat{=}}t_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{1}}}$}\,\ldots\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{n}}}$}\,t_{n}

be its
refinement
in
\rho
.
If
x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y
is not in some
type
of the
profile
of
b

(or equivalently, of
b^{\prime}
), leave it as is.
Otherwise, let
i
(resp.
j
) be the unique
index (by acyclicity) such that

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}[t_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{1}}}$}\,\ldots\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{n}}}$}\,t_{n}]}

leaves

b

at
f(t_{i})
(resp.
leaves

b^{\prime}
at
f(t_{j})
).
Define

\pi^{\prime}(x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y)\mathrel{\hat{=}}t_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{1}}}$}\,\ldots\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{i}}}$}\,t_{i}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}[}L_{i+1}\cdots L_{j}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}]}}}$}\,t_{j}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{j+1}}}$}\,\ldots\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L_{n}}}$}\,t_{n}

when
i\leqslant j
and otherwise the definition is symmetric.
Then, let
\rho^{\prime}
be the
refinement
of
\gamma
obtained by simultaneously
substituting
\pi(x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y)
with
\pi^{\prime}(x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y)
in
\rho
,
for every
atom

x\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,y
of
\gamma
.

Then, let
\alpha^{\prime}
be the query obtained by first
adding the atoms

f(t_{i})\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}[}L_{i+1}\cdots L_{j}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}]}}}$}\,f(t_{j}),

and observe that
f\colon\rho\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}\hskip-8.00003pt\xrightarrow{}$}}}\alpha

induces a
homomorphism

f^{\prime}\colon\rho^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\alpha^{\prime}
—in particular, note that
because of assumption (4) of our claim, we could not have removed images of
free variables of
\gamma
.
Moreover, by construction,
\alpha\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\alpha^{\prime}
(by
2
).
As usual, we restrict
\alpha^{\prime}
to the image of
f^{\prime}
so that it
becomes
strong onto
, while preserving that fact that
\alpha\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\alpha^{\prime}
.
Finally, we build a
tagged tree decomposition

(T^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}^{\prime})

of
f^{\prime}
by applying
Proposition

4.3
; it can be applied because:

•

by assumption
(1)
and
(2)
of the claim, both
bags
have the same cardinality
m\leqslant k
;

•

the variables in common between the first and second half of the
decomposition
are necessarily included in
Z\mathrel{\hat{=}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b)\cap{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b^{\prime})

since we started from a
tree decomposition
;

•

we only add atoms from
x_{i}
to
y_{i}
: depending on whether
x_{i}\in^{?}Z
,
and whether
y_{i}\in^{?}Z
, we fall in one of the four types of atoms allowed
by
Proposition

4.3
.

This concludes the proof of
Section

5.2
.

(a)

Non-branching path
in the “new”
fine tagged tree decomposition
.

(b)

The original query
\gamma
of
tree-width
3.

(c)

The “new”
refinement

\rho^{\prime}
of
\gamma
.

(d)

The “new”
approximation

\alpha^{\prime}
of
tree-width
2.

Figure 13
.

Trio
resulting from applying
Section

5.2

between the second and last
bags
of
Figure

11
.

In
Figure

13(a)
, we depict the
non-branching path
(the rest
of the
fine tree decomposition

Fact 11
.

Let

For every natural number

(p_{i})_{0\leqslant i<m}\in\tilde{P}^{m}
containing at most

t

Proof 5.7
.

First extract from
(x_{i})_{0\leqslant i<m}
the subsequence of elements
distinct from
avoid
, of length at least
\lceil\frac{m}{2}\rceil\geqslant(t+1)d(n+1)+t

Then extract from it contiguous subsequences that avoid
the
trap

t+1

one of them must have size at least
d(n+1)
. Denote by

(y_{i})_{0\leqslant i<d(n+1)}
the prefix of such a subsequence.
Applying the pigeon-hole principle
to
(y_{i\cdot d})_{0\leqslant i<n+1}
yields the desired result.

The next four sections are independent of one another:

•

, we show that the

can be dropped down to

under assumptions on the regular languages;

•

and
