are regular languages over
\mathbb{A}
.
We reduce it to the following problem:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\footnotesize\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}()\mathrel{\hat{=}}\footnotesize\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\footnotesize\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}^{?}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\footnotesize\bullet}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\footnotesize\bullet=\delta^{\prime}().}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\footnotesize\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\#}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\#}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\#}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{1}}
⋮
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\#}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\#}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\#}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\#}

where the right-hand side of
\delta^{\prime}
is a directed
(k+2)
-clique
and where

is a new symbol, i.e.

.

We claim that
\gamma\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\delta
if and only if
\gamma^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\delta^{\prime}
.
The forward implication is direct and the converse implication
simply relies on the fact that
\#\not\in\mathbb{A}
.
15
15

15

Indeed, the only possible
homomorphisms
from

of
\delta^{\prime}
to

of

are the ones sending the
expansions
of
atoms
containing
L_{1},\dotsc,L_{p}
inside the
expansion
of the
atom
on
K
.

Then, observe that
\gamma^{\prime}
has
tree-width

1\leqslant k
,
and that
\delta^{\prime}
is connected but do not have
semantic tree-width

at most
k
.

To prove the last point, consider a
UC2RPQ

\Delta^{\prime\prime}
that is
equivalent to
\delta^{\prime}
. Pick any
expansion

\xi^{\prime}_{1}
of
\delta^{\prime}
.
Since
\Delta^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\delta^{\prime}
, there exists
an
expansion

\xi^{\prime\prime}
of
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
such that there is a
homomorphism

from
\xi^{\prime}_{1}
to
\xi^{\prime\prime}
. Dually, since
\delta^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\Delta^{\prime\prime}
,
there exists an
expansion

\xi^{\prime}_{2}
of
\delta^{\prime}
such that there
is a
homomorphism
from
\xi^{\prime\prime}\to\xi^{\prime}_{2}
. Overall, we have

homomorphisms

\xi^{\prime}_{1}\to\xi^{\prime\prime}\to\xi^{\prime}_{2}
.
Since
\xi^{\prime}_{1}
and
\xi^{\prime}_{2}
are both
expansions
of
\delta^{\prime}
,
they contain a
\#
-labelled directed
(k+2)
-clique,
and the
\#
-letter appears nowhere else.
Should the
homomorphism

\xi^{\prime}_{1}\to\xi^{\prime\prime}
not be injective,

\xi^{\prime\prime}
would contain a
\#
-labelled self-loop,
and hence, the
homomorphism

\xi^{\prime\prime}\to\xi^{\prime}_{2}
would
yield a
\#
-self loop in
\xi^{\prime}_{2}
, which does not exist!
Hence, the
homomorphism
from
\xi^{\prime}_{1}
to
\xi^{\prime\prime}

is injective on the
(k+2)
-clique.
As a result,
\xi^{\prime\prime}
contains a
(k+2)
-clique
and has
tree-width
at least
k+1
.
We conclude that
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
has
tree-width
at least
k+1
by
3
, provided that
k\geqslant 2
.

Hence, we have shown that
\gamma\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\delta
if and only if
\gamma^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\delta^{\prime}

where
\gamma^{\prime}
has
tree-width
at most
k
, where
\delta^{\prime}
is
connected
and
has
semantic tree-width
at least
k+1
. Since our reduction
can be implemented in polynomial time, we conclude that the problems of

22
are
ExpSpace
-hard.

10.
Discussion

10.1.
Complexity

We have studied the definability and approximation of
UC2RPQ
queries by queries of bounded
tree-width
and shown that the
maximal under-approximation
in terms of an infinitary union of
conjunctive queries
of
tree-width

k
can be always effectively expressed as a
UC2RPQ

of
tree-width

k
(
Section

1.3
).
However, while the
semantic tree-width
1
problem
is shown to be
ExpSpace
-complete (which was also established in
[
BRV16
, Theorem 6.1, Proposition 6.2]
), we have left a gap between our lower and upper bounds in
Theorem

1.2
for every
k>1
.

{qu}

For
k>1
, is the
semantic tree-width
k
problem

ExpSpace
-complete?

A related question is whether the
containment problem
between a
C2RPQ
and a
summary query
is in
ExpSpace
. Should this be the case, then the
semantic tree-width
k
problem
would be in
ExpSpace
.
We also point out that since every
path-
l
approximation
can be expressed by a polynomial
UC2RPQ
of tree-width
2k
—this is the same idea as in
[
RBV17
, Lemma IV.13]
—, one can produce, for every
UC2RPQ

\Delta
a union
\Gamma
of poly-sized
C2RPQ
of tree-width
2k
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}(\Delta)}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\Gamma\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\Delta
. This implies that the following ‘‘promise’’ problem
16
16

16

In reference to “promise constraint satisfaction problems”

[
BG21
, Definition 2.3]
.
is decidable in
ExpSpace
: given a
UC2RPQ

\Gamma
, answer ‘yes’ if
\Gamma
is of
semantic tree-width

2k
, and answer ‘no’ if
\Gamma
is not of
semantic tree-width

k
. The fact that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textnormal{App}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-1.04996ptw}_{k\!}}}(\Delta)}
can be approximated by an exponential query of tree-width
2k+1
can also be seen as a corollary of the proof of
[
RBV17
, Theorem V.1]
.

We also do not know whether the
\Pi^{p}_{2}
bound on the
semantic tree-width
k
problem
for
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{UCRPQ}(\textup{SRE})}
has a matching lower bound. The known lower bound for the
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{UCRPQ}(\textup{SRE})}

containment problem

[
FGK+20
, Theorem 5.1]
does not seem to be useful to be employed in a reduction in this context, since it necessitates queries of arbitrary high
tree-width
.

10.2.
Characterization of Tractability

Our result implies that for each
k

the
evaluation problem
for
UC2RPQs

\Gamma

of
semantic tree-width

k
is fixed-parameter tractable—or
FPT
—taking
the query as parameter,
i.e.
, in time
\mathcal{O}(|G|^{c}\cdot f(|\Gamma|))

for a computable function
f
and constant
c
, where
G
is the
database
given as input.
While this was a known fact
[
RBV17
, Corollary IV.12]
, the dependence on the database was
c=2k+1
. Our results show that the dependence can be improved to
c=k+1
, similarly to
[
BRV16
, Theorem 6.3]
for the case
k=1
.
It has been further shown by Feier, Gogacz and Murlak that the
evaluation
can be done with a single-exponential
f

[
FGM24
, Theorem 22]
.

In a similar vein, our results show that the
evaluation problem
for
UC2RPQs

of
semantic path-width

k
is in
paraNL
. It is unknown whether the semantic bounded width properties characterize all
FPT
and
paraNL
classes.

{qu}

Does every recursively enumerable class of
CRPQs
with
paraNL
evaluation have bounded
semantic path-width
?

{qu}

[Also mentioned in
[
RBV17
, §IV-(4)]
]
Does every recursively enumerable class of
CRPQs
with
FPT
evaluation have bounded
semantic tree-width
?

Note that the classes of bounded
contracted path-width
or
contracted tree-width
are not counterexamples to
Sections

10.2
and
10.2
, since
the
path-width
is upper-bounded by one plus the
contracted path-width
, and lower-bounded by the
contracted path-width
—and similarly for
tree-width
—and so a width is bounded
iff
its
contracted
variant is bounded.

In the case of
CQs
, the answer is ‘yes’ to
Section

10.2

[
Gro07
, Theorem 1]

under standard complexity-theoretic hypotheses (
W[1]\neq

FPT
). For
Section

10.2
, the answer is still ‘yes’
[
CM13
, Theorem 3.1]
conditional to a less standard assumption
17
17

17

By
[
CM13
, Theorems 3.1 & 4.3]
, if the class has bounded
semantic path-width
, then the problem is in
\textsf{Path}\subseteq

paraNL
; by
[
CM13
, Theorems 3.1 & 5.5]
, if the class does not have bounded
semantic path-width
, then the problem is
Tree
-hard.
(no
Tree
-hard problem is in
paraNL
).

However, attempting at answering these questions for
CRPQs
is considerably more challenging. In particular, one important technical difficulty is that a class of
CRPQs
with unbounded
tree-width
may contain queries with no
expansions
which are
maximal
in the sense of
containment
. That is, for every
k
, for every query
\gamma
of
semantic tree-width

>k
and
expansion

\xi
of
semantic tree-width

>k
there may be another
expansion

\xi^{\prime}
such that
\xi^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\xi
(
i.e.
, such that
\xi\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\xi^{\prime}
).
In fact, for classes of
CRPQs
avoiding such problematic behavior, Question
10.2
can be positively answered. We next show why.

Let us call a
UC2RPQ

finitely-redundant
if there is no infinite chain
\xi_{1}(\bar{x})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subsetneqq}}\xi_{2}(\bar{x})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subsetneqq}}\dotsb
among its
expansions
. See
Figure

17
for a non-example.

Figure 17
.
(i)
A simple non-
finitely-redundant
Boolean
CRPQ
.
(ii)
For every
n
, there is a
homomorphism
from the
(n+1)
-
expansion
to the
n
-
expansion
, but no
homomorphism
in the converse direction.

Observe that the classes of
CQs
and
UCQs
are
finitely-redundant
, and also the class of
CRPQs
with “
no directed cycles
”, meaning no directed cycle in its underlying directed graph and no empty word
\varepsilon
in the
atom
languages.

Lemma 24
.

The class of
CRPQs
with
no directed cycles
is
finitely-redundant
.

Proof 10.1
.

By means of contradiction, let
\gamma
have
no directed cycles
and suppose there is an infinite chain
\xi_{1}(\bar{x})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subsetneqq}}\xi_{2}(\bar{x})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subsetneqq}}\dotsb
of
expansions
of
\gamma
. Hence, there must be an
atom

expansion
which grows arbitrarily in the chain. Take
\xi_{i}
such that it contains an
atom

expansion
of size bigger than
\xi_{1}
. Since such
atom

expansion
is a directed path (as we are dealing with one-way
CRPQs
), the fact that
\xi_{i}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\xi_{1}
implies that there is some cycle in
\xi_{1}
.
Since
\gamma
cannot contain the empty word in the
atom
languages, this is in contradiction with the hypothesis that there are
no directed cycles
in
\gamma
.

We next show that, restricted to classes of
finitely-redundant

UC2RPQ
, we can obtain a characterization of
evaluation
in
FPT
.

\thmtractabilityfinred

Proof 10.2
.

Left-to-right
By contraposition, we show that if
\mathcal{C}
has unbounded
tree-width
, then its
evaluation problem
is
W[1]
-hard via an
FPT
-reduction from the

parameterized clique problem
. This is the problem of, given a parameter
k
and a simple graph
G
, whether
G
contains a
k
-clique. We do this by a simple adaptation of the proof of Grohe
[
Gro07
, Theorem 4.1]
for the case of
CQs
.

Given an instance
\langle G,k\rangle
of the
parameterized clique problem
, the idea is to first search for a query
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}
of “sufficiently large”
semantic tree-width
.

Let us call an
expansion

\xi
of a
UC2RPQ
to be
maximal
if there is no other
expansion

\xi^{\prime}
such that
\xi\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subsetneqq}}\xi^{\prime}
.
The
core
of a
CQ
is the result of repeatedly removing any atom which results in an equivalent query. It is unique up to isomorphism (see, e.g.,
[
CM77
]
), and a
CQ
has
semantic tree-width

k
iff its
core
has
tree-width

k

[
DKV02
, Theorem 12]
. We say that a
CQ
is ‘a core’ if it is isomorphic to its
core
.

Proposition 10.3
.

For any
k\geqslant 3
, if a
finitely-redundant

C2RPQ
has
semantic tree-width

\geqslant k
, then there is a
maximal

expansion
thereof of
semantic tree-width

\geqslant k
.

Proof 10.4
.

Let
\gamma
be a
finitely-redundant

C2RPQ
.
Consider the
infinitary UCQ

\Xi\mathrel{\hat{=}}\{\mathop{\textrm{core}}(\xi)\mid\xi\text{ is a {\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}maximal} {\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}expansion} of }\gamma\}.

Since
\gamma
is
finitely-redundant
, we have
\gamma\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\equiv}}\Xi
.
We prove the fact by contraposition.
If all
maximal

expansions
of
\gamma
have
semantic tree-width

\leqslant k-1
,
then all
CQs
of
\Xi
have
tree-width

\leqslant k-1
, and so
by the implication
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{{(1)}}}\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textup{{(3)}}}
of
Section

1.3
, query
\gamma
has
semantic tree-width
at most
k-1
.
Note that for
Section

1.3
to apply, we need
k-1>1

i.e.
k\geqslant 3
.

Proposition 10.5
.

The set of all
maximal

expansions
of queries from
\mathcal{C}
is recursively enumerable.

Proof 10.6
.

We first show that given an
expansion

\xi
of some
C2RPQ

\gamma
, it is
decidable whether
\xi
is
maximal
. This follows from the following claim:
there exists an
expansion

\xi^{\prime}
of
\gamma
s.t.
\xi^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\xi
and

\xi\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\not\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\xi^{\prime}
iff
there exists such an
expansion
whose
atom expansions
have length at most

2^{m}\cdot|\xi|\cdot|\mathbb{A}|^{2|\xi|}
where
|\xi|
is the number of variables of
\xi
and

m
is the greatest number of states of an NFA labelling an
atom
of
\gamma
.
Decidability of
maximality
clearly follows from this claim: it suffices to check if

\xi^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\xi
implies
\xi\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\xi^{\prime}
for all “small”
\xi^{\prime}
.

To prove the claim, let
\xi^{\prime}
be an
expansion
of
\gamma
, and assume that there is
a homomorphism
f\colon\xi^{\prime}\to\xi
and that
\xi\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\not\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\xi^{\prime}
. Consider an
atom expansion

\pi^{\prime}=x_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a_{1}}}$}\,x_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a_{2}}}$}\,\cdots\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a_{n-1}}}$}\,x_{n-1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a_{n}}}$}\,x_{n}

of
\xi^{\prime}
, and let
\mathcal{A}
denote the NFA associated with the
atom
.
For any index
i\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\llbracket}0,n{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\rrbracket}
which is neither among the
|\xi|
first positions
nor the
|\xi|
last positions, define its type
\tau_{i}
as
the word
a_{i-|\xi|}-1\cdots a_{i}a_{i+1}\cdots a_{i+|\xi|}
of length
2|\xi|
—note that
\tau_{i}
uniquely describes the ball of radius
|\xi|
centred at
x_{i}
in
\xi
.
Consider the function which maps index
i\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\llbracket}|\xi|,n-|\xi|+1{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\rrbracket}

to the pair
\langle f(x_{i}),Q_{i},\tau_{i}\rangle
, where
Q_{i}
is the set of states
q

of
\mathcal{A}
which admit a path from an initial state to
q
labelled by
a_{1}\cdots a_{i}
.
If
n\geqslant|\xi|\cdot 2^{|\mathcal{A}|}\cdot|\mathbb{A}|^{2|\xi|}+2|\xi|
then by the pigeon-hole principle,
there exists
i,j\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\llbracket}|\xi|,n-|\xi|+1{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\rrbracket}
s.t.
i<j
,
f(x_{i})=f(x_{j})
,

Q_{i}=Q_{j}
and
\tau_{i}=\tau_{j}
. Letting

\pi^{\prime\prime}=x_{0}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a_{1}}}$}\,x_{1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a_{2}}}$}\,\cdots\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a_{i-1}}}$}\,x_{i-1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a_{i}}}$}\,x_{i}=x_{j}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a_{j+1}}}$}\,x_{j+1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a_{j+2}}}$}\,\cdots\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a_{n-1}}}$}\,x_{n-1}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{a_{n}}}$}\,x_{n},

consider the query
\xi^{\prime\prime}
obtained from
\xi^{\prime}

by replacing
\pi^{\prime}
with
\pi^{\prime\prime}
.
Since
Q_{i}=Q_{j}
,
\xi^{\prime\prime}
is still an
expansion
of
\gamma
.
Moreover,
f(x_{i})=f(x_{j})
implies that there is a
homomorphism
from
\xi^{\prime\prime}
to
\xi
.
Lastly, it there was a
homomorphism
from
\xi
to
\xi^{\prime\prime}
,
then this homomorphism should contain
x_{i}
in its image—otherwise there would clearly be a
homomorphism
from
\xi
to
\xi^{\prime}
. Note that the image of this
homomorphism

is included in the ball of
\xi^{\prime\prime}
centered at
x_{i}=x_{j}
of radius
|\xi|
.
But since
\tau_{i}=\tau_{j}
this ball is equal to the ball of
\xi^{\prime}
centered at
x_{i}

(or equivalently at
x_{j}
) of radius
|\xi|
, and so we found a
homomorphism
from

\xi
to
\xi^{\prime}
, which is not possible. Hence, there cannot be any
homomorphism
from

\xi
to
\xi^{\prime\prime}
, which concludes the proof.

Finally, to enumerate all
maximal

expansions
of queries from
\mathcal{C}
,
it suffices to enumerate all
expansions
of queries from
\mathcal{C}
—which is doable since
\mathcal{C}
is recursively enumerable—and only keep those which are
maximal
, using the previous algorithm.

We proceed with the reduction.
For any value of
k
which is big enough, we enumerate all
maximal

expansions
of
\mathcal{C}
until we find one such
expansion

\xi
whose
core
contains a
K\times K
grid as a minor, for
K={k\choose 2}
.
We know that this must happen by
Proposition

10.3
and the Excluded Minor Theorem
[
RS86
]
, stating that there exists a function
f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
every graph of
tree-width
at least
f(n)
contains a
(n\times n)
-grid as a minor.
Once we get hold of such a
maximal

expansion

\xi
, we proceed as in
[
Gro07
, proof of Theorem 4.1]
to produce, in polynomial time, a
graph database

G_{\xi}
such that:

(1)

there is a
homomorphism

G_{\xi}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\xi
, and

(2)

G_{\xi}

satisfies

\xi
if, and only if,
G
has a clique of size
k
.

Now consider the
UC2RPQ

\Gamma\in\mathcal{C}
of which
\xi
is an expansion, and observe that if
G_{\xi}

satisfies

\Gamma
, then we must have that
G_{\xi}
also
satisfies

\xi
, by the fact that
G_{\xi}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\xi
and
\xi
is
maximal
. Hence, the following are equivalent:

•

G_{\xi}

satisfies

\Gamma
,

•

G_{\xi}

satisfies

\xi
,

•

G
contains a
k
-clique.

This finishes the
FPT
-reduction.

Right-to-left
This direction does not need any of the hypotheses (neither
finite-redundancy
,
\textup{W[1]}\neq

FPT
, nor r.e.), by
Section

1.3
.

10.3.
Larger Classes

A natural and simple approach to extend the expressive power of
CRPQs
is to close the queries by transitive closure. That is, given a binary
CRPQ

\gamma(x,y)
we can consider
CRPQ
over the extended alphabet
\mathbb{A}\cup\{\gamma\}
, where the label
\gamma
is interpreted as the binary relation defined by
\gamma(x,y)
. This is the principle behind
Regular Queries

[
RRV17
]
. The notion of
tree-width
can be easily lifted to this class, and classes of bounded
tree-width
still have a polynomial-time
evaluation problem
. However, this class has not yet been studied in the context of the
semantic tree-width
. It is not known if the
semantic tree-width
k
problem
is decidable, nor whether classes of bounded
semantic tree-width
have an
FPT

evaluation problem
.

{qu}

Is the
semantic tree-width
k
problem
for
Regular Queries
decidable?

Different Notions

[

, Theorem 1.3, p. 2]

(
Lemma

8.10
)

\leqslant k

&

(
Section

1.3
)

(

)

\leqslant k

L

[
EJT10
, Lemma 1.4]

P

(Folklore)
18
18

18

Originally proven by Chekuri & Rajaraman
[
CR00
, Theorem 3]
for CQs. The generalization to
UC2RPQs
is trivial, see
e.g.

or
[
RBV17
, Theorem IV.3]
.

sem. tree-w.

Appendix A
Polynomial-Time Evaluation of Queries of Bounded Tree-Width

See
1

Proof A.1
.

A
semi-join
is a
CQ
of the form
q(\bar{x})=R(\bar{x})\land S(\bar{y})
, noted
R(\bar{x})\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\ltimes}S(\bar{y})
, where
\bar{x}
and
\bar{y}
may contain common variables and constants.
Yannakakis algorithm

[
Yan81
]
allows to evaluate any Boolean
acyclic
21
21

21

An
acyclic

CQ
over arbitrary relational structures is one which admits a
tree decomposition
whose set of
bags
are the sets of variables of its atoms (also known as “generalized hyper tree-width 1” or “
\alpha
-acyclicity” of its underlying hypergraph).

conjunctive query

q
on an
n
-tuple relational database in
\mathcal{O}(|q|\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}c_{sj}}(n))
, where
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}c_{sj}}(n)
is the cost of performing a
semi-join
:
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot\log(n))
for Turing machines, or
\mathcal{O}(n)
for a RAM model.
22
22

22

We use Random Access Machines (RAMs) with domain
\mathbb{N}
, in which we assume that the RAM’s memory is initialized to 0.
For every fixed dimension
d\geqslant 1
we have available an unbounded number of
d
-ary arrays
A
such that for given
(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
the entry
A[n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d}]
at position
(n_{1},\dotsc,n_{d})
can be accessed in constant time.
To compute
R(\bar{x}\,\bar{y}){\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}S(\bar{y}\,\bar{z})
, where
\bar{y}
are the only common variables between the atoms and
n_{R}
and
n_{S}
are the number of tuples in
R
and
S
respectively, we may assume that constants from the relations are encoded as numbers in binary, of size
\mathcal{O}(\log(n_{R}+n_{S}))
.
We first encode the relation
R
projected onto
\bar{y}
, as an array
A
of the dimension of
\bar{y}
in
\mathcal{O}(n_{R})
, by putting a ‘1’ in
A
for each tuple of
R
.
Then, for each tuple of
S
we test if the
\bar{y}
-projection belongs to
A
, if so we put a ‘2’ in the array
A
, this is
\mathcal{O}(n_{S})
.
Finally, for each tuple of
R
we output the tuple if its projection onto
\bar{y}
has a ‘2’ in
A
. This last step takes
\mathcal{O}(n_{R})
.

We reduce the
evaluation problem
for
C2RPQs
of
tree-width

k
to the
evaluation problem
of Boolean acyclic
CQs
. First, if we receive
\Gamma(\bar{x})
,
G
and
\bar{v}
as input, we replace the
free variables

\bar{x}
of
\Gamma
with the
graph database
nodes
\bar{v}
as constants, to obtain a Boolean
C2RPQ
(with constants). Let us therefore assume that
\Gamma
is Boolean.
Take any
\gamma\in\Gamma
and a
tagged tree decomposition

(T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}})
of
width
at most
k
of
\gamma
.
23
23

23

By a
tagged tree decomposition
of
\gamma
we mean one for the identity
\gamma\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\raisebox{-0.89996pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{\textit{\tiny hom}}}$}}}\gamma
. The definition of
tagged tree decompositions
can be found in
Section

4
.

It is easy to see that we can assume that the decomposition is of linear size in the number of
atoms

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|_{\textrm{at}}}
.
24
24

24

Indeed, observe that the decomposition resulting from contracting any edge so that one
bag
is contained or equal to the other is of linear size and preserves the
width
(see,
e.g.
,
[
GLS02
]
).

For every
bag

v
of the decomposition containing
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(v)=\{x_{1},\dotsc,x_{t}\}
consider the following relation
R_{v}
consisting of all tuples of
database
nodes
(v_{1},\dotsc,v_{t})
such that for every
atom

A=x_{i}\,\raisebox{-0.91995pt}{$\xrightarrow{\smash{L}}$}\,x_{j}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}^{-1}(v)
we have that
(v_{i},v_{j})

satisfies

A
.

It then follows that

(1)

each
R_{v}
is of size
\mathcal{O}(|G|^{k+1})
,

(2)

the
Boolean

CQ

q=\bigwedge_{v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V}(T)}R_{v}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(v))
is
acyclic
and of size linear in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|_{\textrm{at}}}
, and

(3)

q
is
equivalent
to
\gamma
.

Hence,
Yannakakis algorithm
yields a complexity of
\mathcal{O}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}c_{sj}}(|G|^{k+1}))
. That is,
\mathcal{O}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\cdot|G|^{k+1}\cdot\log{|G|})
, or
\mathcal{O}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\cdot|G|^{k+1})
in a RAM model.

We are only left with the cost of computing the relation
R_{v}
for any given
v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V}(T)
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{v}=\{A_{1}(y_{1},z_{1}),\dotsc,A_{s}(y_{s},z_{s})\}
be the set of all
atoms
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}^{-1}(v)
. Let
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(v)=\{x_{1},\dotsc,x_{t}\}
.
Compute first the relation
S_{i}=\{(u,u^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V}(G)^{2}:(u,u^{\prime})\text{ {\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}satisfies} }A_{i}(y_{i},z_{i})\}
for every
i
; this can be done in
\mathcal{O}(|G|\cdot|L_{i}\times{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G^{\pm}}|)
, where
L_{i}\times{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G^{\pm}}
is the product of the NFA for the regular language
L_{i}
of
A_{i}
and the expanded
database

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G^{\pm}}
.
25
25

25

That is,
L_{i}\times{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G^{\pm}}
is a
database
having pairs
(q,v)
where
q
is a state for the NFA
\mathcal{A}_{i}
of
L_{i}
and
v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G^{\pm}})
, and we have an edge
(q,v)\xrightarrow{a}(q^{\prime},v^{\prime})
in

if
q\xrightarrow{a}q^{\prime}
and
v\xrightarrow{a}v^{\prime}
are in
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G^{\pm}}

Compute also the relation
U={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V}(G)^{t}
in

(hence in
\mathcal{O}(|G|^{k+1})
). Finally, we compute

by performing
s

((U(x_{1},\dotsc,x_{t}){\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}S_{1}(y_{1},z_{1})){\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}S_{2}(y_{2},z_{2})\dotsb){\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}S_{s}(y_{s},z_{s})

in

, that is, in

or

\mathcal{O}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\cdot|G|^{k+1}\cdot\log(|G|))
(observe that we iterate only once on each

\mathcal{O}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\cdot|G|^{k+1})
in a RAM model.

Appendix B
Alternative Upper Bound for Containment of UC2RPQs

In
Section

3
, in order to prove the
2ExpSpace
upper bound to the

semantic tree-width
k
problem
(
4
), we proved an upper bound on containment
of
UC2RPQs
(
Proposition

3.10
) by relying on the notion of
bridge-width
.
In this section, we give a slightly different bound, which is more elementary (in the sense that it
does not rely on
bridge-width
) and still yields a
2ExpSpace
upper bound to the
semantic
tree-width
k
problem
.

Proposition B.1
.

The
containment problem

\Gamma\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\subseteqq}}\Delta
between two
UC2RPQs
can be solved in non-deterministic space
2^{c\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}}+p_{\Delta}\cdot 2^{c\cdot m_{\Delta}}
, where
m_{\Delta}
is the size of the greatest disjunct of
\Delta
, namely
m_{\Delta}=\max{\{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\delta_{\Delta}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\mid\delta\in\Delta\}}
,
p_{\Delta}

is the number of disjuncts of
\Delta
, and
c
is a constant.

Proof B.2

(Proof sketch)
.

The proposition can be shown by close inspection of the standard
containment problem
for
UC2RPQs

[
CDLV00
, Theorem 5]
: the
containment problem
is reduced, in this instance, to checking the inclusion between NFAs of the form
26
26

26

\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\delta}
are denoted
A_{1}
and
A_{3}
, respectively, in
[
CDLV00
]
.

\immediate\immediate\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}\subseteq^{?}\bigcup_{\delta\in\Delta}\mathcal{A}_{\delta},

(7)

where
A_{\Gamma}
is a regular expression which is exponential in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}
,
and
\mathcal{A}_{\delta}
has size exponential in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\delta{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\leqslant m_{\Delta}
.
Should (
7
) not hold, there must exist a counterexample of size at most

2^{|\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}|}\times\prod_{\delta\in\Delta}2^{|\mathcal{A}_{\delta}|}

Letting
p_{\Delta}
be the number
of queries in
\Delta
, we get that the logarithm of the expression above—representing the
size of the non-deterministic space needed by the algorithm—is upper bounded by

c_{0}\Bigl(|\mathcal{A}_{\Gamma}|+\sum_{\delta\in\Delta}|\mathcal{A}_{\delta}|\Bigr)\mathrel{\underset{\tiny\textrm{eventually}}{\leqslant}}2^{c\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\|}}+p_{\Delta}\cdot 2^{c\cdot n_{\Delta}},

for some constants
c_{0}
and
c
.

Appendix C
Path-Width is not Closed under Refinements

See
19

Proof C.1
.

Let
X
be a set of
k-1
variables. Consider the undirected multigraph
\mathcal{G}_{k}
whose set of nodes is

X\cup\{y_{0},y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\}
with the following edge set:

•

each
X\cup\{y_{i}\}
(
i\in\{0,1,2,3\}
) is a clique,

•

there is an edge from
y_{i}
to
y_{i+1}
for
i\in\{0,1,2\}
, and

•

there is a second edge from
y_{1}
to
y_{2}
.

By definition, this graph has
path-width
exactly
k
: it is as least
k
since it contains
a
(k+1)
-clique—namely
X\cup\{y_{i},y_{i+1}\}
—and, moreover the following sequence of bags—cf.
Figure

15(a)
—defines a
path decomposition
of
\mathcal{G}_{k}
of
width

k
:

\langle X\cup\{y_{0},y_{1}\},\;X\cup\{y_{1},y_{2}\},\;X\cup\{y_{2},y_{3}\}\rangle.

Let
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{k}
be the graph obtained by
refining
the second edge from
y_{1}
to
y_{2}
, into
two edges
\langle y_{1},z\rangle
and
\langle z,y_{2}\rangle
, where
z
is a new variable—see
Figures

15(b)
and
15(d)
.
We claim that
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{k}
has path-width at least
k+1
. Indeed, let

\langle T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}\rangle
be a
path decomposition
of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{k}
.

Note that
X\cup\{y_{0},y_{1}\}
,
X\cup\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
,
X\cup\{y_{2},y_{3}\}

and
\{z,y_{1},y_{2}\}
are cliques, so there must be
bags
of
\langle T,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{t}}\rangle
containing each of them. Let
b_{0,1}
,
b_{1,2}
,
b_{2,3}
and
b_{Z}
denote these bags—note that they do not have to be distinct.

(1)

If
b_{Z}
appears in
T
between at least two
bags
among
b_{0,1}
,
b_{1,2}

and
b_{2,3}
(as in
Figure

15(b)
), since
X\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b_{i,j})
for all
(i,j)
, then
X\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b_{z})
.
Hence
X\cup\{z,y_{1},y_{2}\}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b_{z})

and so
b_{z}
has
k+2
elements.

(2)

Otherwise, w.l.o.g.
b_{z}
appears strictly before all three bags

b_{0,1}
,
b_{1,2}
and
b_{2,3}
, as in
Figure

15(d)
.
We consider the way
b_{0,1}
,
b_{1,2}
and
b_{2,3}
are ordered in the
path decomposition
. If
b_{0,1}
or
b_{2,3}
appears first, then
they are located between
b_{z}
and
b_{1,2}
, which both contain
\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
,
and so this bag must also contain
\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
, and so it has size at least
k+2
.
Otherwise, if
b_{1,2}
appears first, depending on the relative ordering
of
b_{0,1}
and
b_{2,3}
, we either get that
y_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b_{0,1})

or that
y_{1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathbf{v}}(b_{2,3})
. In both cases, we have a bag with at least

k+2
elements.

In all cases, the
path decomposition
has width at least
k+1
, showing that

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{k}
has
path-width
at least
27
27

27

In fact it has
path-width
exactly
k+1
.

k+1
.
